# Nobody Marries the List: Book Plan

Last updated: Friday, October 16, 2026, 5:41 PM PDT. Chapters 1 to 29 drafted.

**How to read this plan.** This is the plan as it stands today, and every section states what is true now. Where a chapter is drafted, the chapter file is the final word and the plan summarizes it. Anything still waiting on Todd is marked "Claude's addition" or listed under Open questions. How the plan got here is in the Decision record at the end; nothing in that record overrides the sections above it. The project file claude/book-plan.md and the doc "Nobody Marries the List: Book Plan" hold the same text.

## The book

- **Title:** Nobody Marries the List. **Pen name:** Wendy Garrity. The Neopulp mark goes on the cover and the copyright page.
- **Shelf:** a romantic comedy for the single-mom, later-in-life shelf, not the billionaire shelf. The billionaire reader wants rescue by money, and this book's black moment makes a paid-off mortgage the betrayal.
- **Form:** first person, past tense, alternating Jo (odd chapters) and Cal (even chapters). About 85,000 words: 36 chapters near 2,300 words each, and an epilogue in Jo's voice.
- **Heat:** 3 of 5. Open door, on the page, direct without being graphic. Three love scenes, in chapters 18, 21 and 35.
- **The genre's style governs** (Todd). The book reads like a romance novel on every page. Sentence rules are in claude/romance-style-guide.md; voices are in claude/character-sheet.md.
- **Kindle Unlimited:** the ebook is exclusive to Amazon for each 90-day term. The book opens a series of interconnected standalones around the matchmaker's office.

**Premise.** Cal Brennan, an AI-software billionaire, asks his own product to read his history and describe his perfect wife. He agrees with all twelve lines and hands the list to a matchmaker. The matchmaker sends him Jo Silveira, a 44-year-old mover's daughter who clears dead people's houses for a living and fails eleven of the lines. The magic happens for him on the first date. The book is him learning to love her as she is, which for this man means loving her without managing, buying or delegating, and he has to learn it in a house he cannot walk into alone.

**Logline.** When the woman a matchmaker sends him fails eleven of the twelve lines on his list and he still cannot end the date, a man who pays other people to do his loving has to show up himself, ten Saturdays in his dead mother's house with her in the room, or lose her and his daughter the way he lost his mother.

## What the book is about

- **Cal's question:** can a man who has outsourced every act of love learn to show up himself? His product remembers, Janice writes, the matchmaker finds, and money solves.
- **Jo's question:** can a woman who leans on nothing that can leave her let someone carry something for her?
- **The engine:** is Cal his history, or can he do something it did not predict? His record, kept by his own agent, is how the reader measures whether he has learned to show up.
- **The black moment is about being known.** He thought the money proved he loved her. She takes it as proof he never knew her.
- **The last chapter proves the change:** a problem he cannot delegate or buy, handled himself.

**Each lead's core** (Todd, October 10: "it wasn't meant to be revolutionary, but grounding"). Each of them has a wound and a picture of peace.
- **Jo: the woman who can carry anything, and leans on nothing that can go** (Todd, October 10; deepened October 16: "Being dependent brings you pain when that thing goes away"). She is so good at handling things alone that nobody has ever carried anything for her. She has watched what happens when the thing a person leans on goes away, in other people's houses on every working day and in her own family. At seven, on her first job with her father, she lay down in the emptied front room of a one-story in Millbrae, in the square where the couch had been, and it was the most peaceful she had ever felt; a man sat in a car at the curb all morning, and when she came out he was gone. At sixteen, on 280, her father told her whose house it was: a bank job; the wife had died the year before, five weeks after the baby (she killed herself; that is held for 34, told to Cal in a line); the husband sat down in a chair and quit work; his mother took the baby, and the bank took the house and paid Frank to empty it. The winter she was eight, her father's knee went and he sat in a chair in the front room until spring, the first time she watched him go quiet. Kevin first went quiet when Owen was five weeks old, in March 2017; she had leaned on him all that winter and never did again, and she was back on a truck the next Monday. When her mother died in February 2019, her father went quiet from the funeral to Connie's birthday. Her rule: lean on nothing that can go. A house she pays off herself is the one thing that cannot go quiet on her; until it is paid she cannot stop or want anything, and the day it was paid she was going to lie down. Peace, to her, is an emptied house, where there is nothing left to lose. On every job the piano is what is left when a house is nearly empty, which is why it is her word for the catch. Chapter 28 does not take money from her. It makes her house lean on him, three months in: from then on, every time she comes in her own front door, she walks into something he gave her, and in 29 his own assistant tells her he always gives a present before the end.
- **Cal: the man who can make anything work.** He can make almost anything happen, and none of it reaches the people he wants to reach: his mother died with a voicemail he has not played, Abby calls him an account manager, and Jo will not take what he sends. Peace, to him, is the second a thing he built does exactly what he planned. As a boy he switched on a science fair project and it did what the drawing said; its ribbon is one of the blue ones his mother put on the shelf in his old room (ch 20, 21). The report cards are why he trusts the right number to make a person speak. He trusts what he can make work and distrusts everything else, including himself with people. The first date is the one thing in his life that worked and that he did not build.
- **What changes.** Jo leans on someone on purpose, knowing he could go, and finds that a job can wait while he stays beside her. Cal finds that staying beside someone matters more than making it work.
- **Where the two pictures go on the page.** Each once, late, in the narrator's own words. Jo's is in 29, when the loan reads zero: the emptied house in Millbrae at seven, and at sixteen whose house it was. Cal's goes where it serves a scene, probably 26 or 36, told to Jo. Neither adds an object: hers rides the slab and her name in 21 ("since I was seven, in an empty house in Millbrae") and the walk-through in 34, where she lies down on the slab in the emptied house and he lies down beside her; his rides the ribbons in 20 and 21.

What each stretch of the book answers:

| Chapters | The question |
|---|---|
| 1 to 9 | Is there something between them? |
| 10 to 15 | Can she trust who is speaking to her? |
| 16 to 24 | Can their two lives fit together? |
| 25 to 29 | Can he stop using money as love? |
| 30 to 35 | Can she lean on someone who could go quiet? |
| 36 | Can they have trouble without turning into their parents? |

What each of them says as they change:

| | Start | Middle | Late | End |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cal | "What is the ask?" (ch 1) | "What do you need?" (ch 21) | "What do you want?" (ch 27) | "Tell me." (ch 34) |
| Jo | "I've got it." (ch 1) | "I can do it." (ch 17) | "Can you?" (ch 25) | "I need you to stay." (ch 35) |

Cal's two middle lines are placed by Claude; Todd may move them.

**Jo's ladder.** She changes on the page before chapter 35, one rung at a time, and each rung is an act. Ch 7: she cannot ask, so she bills him a dinner. Ch 17: "I can do it," and then she lets him make the breakfast. Ch 25: "Can you?", the first thing she asks him for that is not a bill. Ch 27: "I've got it" costs somebody else, her father. Ch 34: she moves the piano that was never on her estimate, with no change order, and hands him the low end and the call, before either of them has said a word about August; she chooses him before he has shown her anything. Ch 35: she sends for him, and then says "I need you to stay." Ch 36: his name on Owen's emergency card, which she asked him about first. She is never his instructor; she is on her own ladder.

**Milestones.**

| Milestone | Chapter | Point in the book |
|---|---|---|
| They meet | 1 | Page one |
| Almost a kiss | 7 | 19% |
| First kiss | 9 | 25% |
| First love scene | 18 | 50% |
| Second love scene | 21 | 58% |
| He says he loves her | 26 | 72% |
| Black moment | 29 | 80% |
| She speaks | 34 | 94% |
| She says it; third love scene | 35 | 97% |

## Story rules

**The romance**
- They share the page in 35 of 36 chapters. The one where they are apart is 33, and it is about him.
- He does not fall in love on the first date. He knows only that he did not want it to end. Jo is attracted at once and refuses to trust it.
- He takes her no. She turns down a second date (ch 3) and he accepts it, offers to take the job to another company, and does not ask again. She reopens it herself in ch 7.
- No other women on the page. He cancels the other introductions on the drive home from the first date.
- Attraction in every chapter, said outright in the narrator's body and never left to be inferred (Todd, on the first draft of chapter 1). It changes as they do, without announcing its stages: first she is physically wrong for his list; then she distracts him; then her competence draws him; then her fear and her nerve undo him; at the end he loves the exact things that once disqualified her.
- Every chapter ends on a change between the two of them (a decision, a touch, a refusal, a promise), on something said or done. No cliffhangers, and no closing thought about what it all means.
- The middle is fun. Every chapter from 4 to 24 has a laugh that comes from the two of them: the boots, the cue card, forty gray shirts, the horseradish, the dog rankings, the wrong lunch, the terrible breakfast, the parking ticket, the missing wallet, a billionaire airsick in his own airship.
- Backstory is told to the other lead, or it waits. A side character's scene needs a lead in the room. The one exception is Jo's core (October 16): the reader has to have the reason by 29, so it is seeded in her own narration (11, 19, 21, 23, 27) and given in 29, and she still tells Cal in 34.
- The title is paid in the epilogue: the cue card is a proposal.
- From chapter 22 on, every romantic scene lets one of them do something they could not have done earlier: he leaves her a decision he would once have made for her, or she takes something from him without working out what she owes (outside critique, October 10). A scene that only shows they want each other more is folded into another scene or cut.
- No new machinery from chapter 19 on (outside critique, October 9). Claude adds no new plants or recurring objects while drafting. New ones come from Todd, and where it can, a new idea rides an existing plant. Not every plant has to pay off at the end; the list and the cue card carry the ending, with the mortgage and the piano under them. Every scene has to work as a scene first.

**Cal**
- He is not a bad man and not neurodivergent (Todd). He is smart, focused, driven and more self-absorbed than is good for him. He reads other people fast and well, which is how he raised money and hired; what he does not trust is himself with them, so he assigns it. Each Cal chapter from 12 on has at least one quick, correct read of another person.
- He does not say he has never felt this before when an act already shows it. The acts are the romance: the phone turned over, the card left in the console, the shrimp, the door, the lunch.
- His reach is a pleasure to watch when he asks before he uses it, and each time the easy fix is refused or turns out wrong: the cruise (6, unasked, and it cannot be fixed), the taco truck (8 and 9, asked, and right), the cool house (21, asked and refused), a paid crew for Frank's boat (23, asked and refused), the review cleanup (25, asked and refused). Hawaii (19) is the one splash she says yes to, because it is for Owen and he asked her first. In ch 12 Jo watches his office run on one sentence from him and is impressed against her will. After chapter 6, anything he does for her unasked is either Remy's plan firing (16, 20) or the relapse (28).
- He wins once on her ground (ch 25): the thing he knows cold helps her, she asks for it, and no money is involved. It makes chapter 28 worse.
- He never says sorry without a fix attached, until the end. He has never ended a relationship out loud; see Remy and the plan.

**Jo**
- She is exact and never cruel, in the fight (ch 13) and in the black moment (ch 29). Nothing about his looks, his mother or his daughter. He does not get a clever comeback.
- Her word for the catch she keeps waiting for is "the piano," the thing that is never on the estimate. She never says "the bill" (Todd).
- She is a fool for him once in each Jo chapter through 17, something she would call stupid in anyone else. Ch 11 has two (she checks her hair under his billboard; she kisses a client in a hall with her son thirty feet away). Ch 13 has two (she laughs out loud alone in his garage at "Noted. That is five"; she slides down behind her wheel when his car comes down the ramp). Ch 15 has one (she sends Vili in the other truck so Cal rides beside her). Ch 17 has one (at 2:10 in the morning she stands at his door with her hand up to knock and a sentence ready, and goes back to the guest room).
- Her "reasons not to" were a running joke (staff, forty shirts, his face on a billboard). They ended at eight, at the first kiss in ch 9, and are not counted again.
- Each time she turns down help from 21 to 28, the refusal shows something new (outside critique, October 10). In 21 she turns down the cold house for his mother's sake ("Your mother lived in it hot"), and it gets a memory out of him, the box fan in his window. In 23 the transmission goes and she tells him nothing, the first need she hides from him instead of refusing. In 25 the reviews have her name on them, and the same night she asks him for something for the first time. In 27 her "I've got it" costs her father his knees, and she says it anyway. In 28 she turns down a truck from a man who has said he loves her and whom she has not answered. No refusal repeats another's reason.
- **Jo on a job** (October 13, after Millbrae in 25 broke the first rule). Every job scene is checked against her working rules before filing, and the independent reviewer gets this list: whoever owns the thing is in the room when it gets decided, so green and blue are the owner's call (2, 3, 11); found items are called, seen and signed for; the crew stops at eleven (25); nothing goes home with her; what a client cannot call stays in the room until he can (21); she does bathrooms by herself, with the door open (11).

**The shape of the ending**
- The mortgage breaks a promise. In ch 17 she makes him promise never to pay for anything of hers without asking. In ch 28 he does it anyway, out of fear, as month four closes in.
- Two absences, and her share. Kevin withdraws when things get hard; Cal takes over. Jo's part is that she never asks. The ending needs both changes: he stays without fixing (ch 34) and she asks (ch 35). When she stops talking in ch 29 she is doing to Cal what Kevin did to her, and what her father did twice. Nobody says so.
- Her silence is self-protection. If she says one more word, it may be the one she did not say on the rock, and that would be putting her whole weight on a man whose assistant has just told her he always gives a present before the end. She knows nothing about his mother's silences. His growth is learning that her silence is not his to solve.
- **What Cal overcomes** (Todd, October 16: "something that cal can overcome to free her"). His way with trouble is to send something and stay away: the boxes to his mother, the eleven he let go quiet, the counselor Janice booked. To free her he does not go quiet while she does. Through her nine days he sends nothing and fixes nothing, and he is at the house every Saturday. He is in the room for the bad things (32, the vet at two; 36, Owen). His name on Owen's emergency card in 36 takes the house's place as what keeps her safe.
- The breakup keeps them in one room: her father's name is on the contract, so she finishes the job.
- No secret survives chapter 33. His letter tells her she was filed as relationship twelve, that he approved a plan unread, that the car and Napa were that plan, and that the plan stopped at the month-four gift because none of the other eleven lasted longer.
- **How dark it gets** (Todd, October 8). The black moment is as dark as the book goes: love in the wrong currency, a broken promise, and something taken that was hers. Remy is cold; Cal is careless, never cruel. Nothing in the plan was written to end things with Jo, and none of the other eleven was ended by a message.
- Abby has her own life. She sees through him now and then; she is not his therapist and not his redemption. The verdict that he was "a really good account manager" comes from Abby, never from Jo.

**The counts**
- His "noted" was counted for the last time in chapter 15, at seven. It comes back once, on the epilogue card.
- Lines struck from the list: six after chapter 14, seven after chapter 21, then eight in 27, nine in 34, ten in 35 and eleven in 36 (see His list). The twelfth is never struck. He owes her half a linguiça sandwich and no lines.
- Janice's pauses: she has paused before answering him eight times in seventeen years. Chapter 2 counts from four to six, the seventh is in ch 10 and the eighth in ch 12. From chapter 16 on there are no running counts on the page except the lines Remy says itself ("Thirteen items remain," "Unanswered: 9 days," "No precedent found"); Cal still notices in numbers when the number is the joke or the fact. The reader should forget the machinery for twenty pages at a time, so that "No precedent found" lands.

## The cast

### Jo Silveira, 44

Owner of Silveira Moving & Estate in South San Francisco: eleven employees, three trucks (one is the sixteen-footer she keeps back for estimates and small jobs), and her father's name on the doors. She lives in San Bruno. She is pretty and has stopped thinking about it: dark hair tied back for work, brown eyes, a wide mouth, sun on her skin, no makeup. Cal says so outright; in her own chapters it shows in how men look at her. On a job the carpenter's pencil lives behind her ear. Divorced three years; custody of Owen week on, week off. Two years at Skyline College, business, before the trucks needed her (ch 14).

- **Wants:** to make payroll, get Owen through fourth grade, and own her house outright by 55 on her own spreadsheet.
- **Under it:** someone to lean on who will not go quiet, who takes the heavy end without sending a bill.
- **Wound:** at seven, the bank job in Millbrae (see Jo's core above). The winter she was eight (1990), her father hurt his knee on a job, and the one truck sat until spring. He sat in a chair in the front room until spring, the first time she watched him go quiet. The church and the neighbors fed the family. A man from the parish paid the truck note for three months, and everybody at church knew who. Her job was to wash the casserole dishes and take them back. Each had its owner's name on a strip of masking tape on the bottom. She wrote every name down, her first found sheet, and she has never told anyone about the list. When Frank came back he carried everything himself. Her rule comes from that winter: everything anybody gives you has a name on the bottom. Kevin's parents were the second time. They gave the down payment and never let them forget it, every Sunday for eleven years. In 2023 she refinanced at seven percent to buy Kevin out and paid his parents back with interest nobody asked for, the way she took back the dishes washed. (The winter is Claude's, adopted by Todd on October 8, after "That can't be Jo's. She was that way before. Why?") The marriage ended in Kevin's silences, and in her never asking him for anything; in eleven years she asked for one thing, a signature. Kevin first went quiet when Owen was five weeks old, in March 2017; she had leaned on him all that winter and never did again. When her mother died in February 2019, her father went quiet from the funeral to Connie's birthday; the first thing he said, coming back under the Golden Gate, was that she'd have been sick right there (ch 23).
- **Rule:** lean on nothing that can go. Nothing given is free.
- **Knows:** families fight over the casserole dish, never the silver. Cash is in the freezer, the Bible and the coat pockets. The low man calls the lift (her father's rule, not the trade's). A job done too fast gets a worse tip. Every found item is called out loud, seen by a second person, written on the found sheet and signed for.
- **Quirk:** she takes nothing home from a job. Her purse holds a tape measure, green tape and a carpenter's pencil.
- **Talks:** short statements, logistics verbs, practical questions as affection. She says "I've got it" and never "I need" until chapter 35. When a subject has to close, she asks a work question.
- **Afraid of dogs:** bitten on a job at 26, eleven stitches in the back of her left calf. She says "I'm not good with dogs" and makes room for this one anyway.
- **Secret:** she broke her own rule once. A recipe tin from a stranger's kitchen sits in her cupboard because it looked like her mother's, which she threw out by mistake.
- **Confidante:** her sister Denise.

### Cal Brennan, 41

Founder of Recall Technologies, whose agent, Remy, runs the work and the errands of forty million paying users. In public he is good-looking, well dressed and cool, the face of his product, and he used all of it on the way up. In private he is smart, focused, driven and more self-absorbed than is good for him; keeping track of people was the first thing he stopped doing, which is why he built Remy and why he pays a person to remember for him. Raised in Sunnyvale by Darlene, in an old Eichler on West Remington Drive; she worked the line at Libby's cannery until it closed in 1985, the year he was born, and then the cafeteria at the middle school across the street for thirty years, where she handed him his tray. Married at 22, a father at 23, divorced at 27. Lives in Woodside. One daughter, Abby. One dog, Pancake.

- **Wants:** a wife in place before Abby leaves, so the house is not empty.
- **Under it:** to be forgiven by Abby without having to ask, and to be again the man he was for three hours on the first date.
- **Wound:** when his mother was hurt she stopped speaking, sometimes for nine days. What ended it was a report card. He would leave it on the kitchen table, she would read it and sign it, and she would start talking again. (She asked what he got on every test and read every report card before she signed it, ch 14; she had left school at sixteen and hid it.) So he learned that the right number gets someone to speak to you. His times to the minute, his counts, specs and tests, the forty shirts, the list and Remy all start there. He has never told anyone about the report cards either. (The report cards are Claude's, adopted by Todd on October 8.) He learned to answer feelings with deliverables. He paid for everything and attended nothing. He has never told anyone about the nine days.
- **Rule:** if it matters, assign it to someone good.
- **Knows:** how a model fails: fluent, confident and wrong. He trusts a spec and a test.
- **Quirk:** he tests and returns things until he finds the one he loves, then buys forty: thirty-one shirts tried, forty of the gray one bought (numbered one to forty, by hand, in ch 14). He is doing this with women and has not noticed.
- **Talks:** whole sentences, never fragments, and no contractions (Voice rules); numbers and readings; "What is the ask?" and "Noted." He has a second voice for the stage, warm, fluent and rehearsed, and when he is nervous with Jo he slips into it; she calls it presenting. His watch taps when his pulse is high.
- **Secret:** his mother's last voicemail, unplayed. He has read only Remy's summary of it.

### Why Remy won

Remy is what the big companies' agents promise: always on, runs for months, works across everything a person owns, and gets things right. Their agents have no face and no taste, and nobody loves them. Remy delivered the promise and Cal made it cool, the way Steve Jobs made a computer something people wanted to be seen with. No real company or person is named in the book.

- **Cal is the brand.** At ease in front of four thousand people, known by one gray shirt.
- **He sold a feeling.** He never says "agent" on a stage; he sells what the time is for. Remy's best-known ad is three words, "Call your mother" (Todd), on a billboard over 101 that Jo drives under. The pitch: Remy does the rest, so you can show up for your people.
- **He got his own product backwards.** He let Remy do the people too and gave the time to work, and his mother's last voicemail is unplayed. The book ends with him using Remy the way his ad says to.
- **He used it on the way up:** investors, press, hires and the women in the photographs. The first four lines of the list are the brand's taste.
- **The gap is the book.** He can hold a hall and cannot hold a table for two. Jo is the first person to see the cue card.
- Claude's addition, not confirmed: two reasons Remy could deliver what the others could not. It had ten years of people's history before the models arrived, so it remembers where the big agents forget; and it asks first, which is why people trust it with everything.

### Everyone else

- **Janice Louie, 61.** His assistant for seventeen years; she was to retire in December (ch 12). She writes his long messages and has written Abby's birthday cards in his handwriting since Abby was four. She crosses her sevens and sets every dial to an even number. She started his cue cards ("I taught him to bring them. The squaring is his."). Her card file has a card for everyone he has met since 2009, two for Jo. Her put-downs are courteous verdicts, and she calls him "Calvin" when he is wrong. She stopped writing to Jo on her own once Jo told her Denise's name (ch 14). She refuses the payoff and gives notice the same afternoon (ch 28). She is gone by September and leaves him the card file (ch 36).
- **Abby Brennan, 18.** His daughter; architecture at Cal Poly, moving in Tuesday, August 18. She wants to build school cafeterias with windows, because her grandmother's had none. Exact, and has had it pointed at him since she was twelve; her exclamation point means a door has closed. Wants to leave clean; under it, for him to ask her to stay one more weekend. She has always known who wrote her birthday cards (ch 22). Her grandmother measured her on the bathroom door from three to fourteen. Her mother, Stephanie, Cal's ex-wife (married 2007 to 2012), hosts the send-off in 22 and is the book's Lady Catherine in 31 (Todd, October 9). Cal has called her X since 2012, in his phone and in his head. He first calls her X on the page in ch 14, and in 22 Abby makes him say her name (Todd, October 9).
- **Owen, 9.** Jo's son. A fan of Cal's; makes his own apps with Remy and loves it, on an older phone his father handed down (Todd). He ranks every dog he meets in an app he wrote himself (Truck Dog first, Pancake second, until ch 19). He goes quiet when he is told no, which he gets from his father. He loves volcanoes the way other kids love dinosaurs: Pompeii at school, a volcano app he nearly built before the dog one, and his grandpa's ash story from 1980 (ch 11 and 19).
- **Dawn Pruitt, 54.** Matchmaker, former divorce lawyer of fifteen years. Wants a perfect record; under it, nobody has asked what she wants in ten years. She sent Jo because everyone else she sent was impressed by him, and because Jo is the one his daughter would call if something happened to him (told to Cal in ch 32). In ch 32 she gives him the rule her own therapist gave her ten years ago, when her marriage ended (from the matchmaker's channel, Todd, October 12): the marriage that fails is the one where the woman loves him more, because only a man who is madly in love does the work, and a woman does it either way. She hated hearing it, and it tells Cal which one she was. She does not use NDAs.
- **Kelsey Dunbar, 27.** Junior matchmaker, former tech recruiter; calls the list "the req" and says "to be fair."
- **Frank Silveira, 74.** Jo's father. Founded Silveira Moving in 1987. Bad knees since the winter of 1990, when he hurt one on a job and the one truck sat until spring; sits on the dock in his chair (on the cushion Jo bought because of a message). Makes her sandwiches on Friday nights. "Low man calls the lift." Asks whether Cal can carry, not whether he can hire. In May 1980, at twenty-eight, he was stranded in Ritzville, Washington, in the Mount St. Helens ash with a load on the truck; Owen has heard it a hundred times (ch 19). His wife, Connie, was born July 27, 1956, and died in 2019. He has kept a 1976 Catalina 27 at Oyster Point since 1994, sits in it at the dock most Sundays after Mass, and takes it out under the Golden Gate once a year, on her birthday; under the bridge he takes his cap off (ch 23).
- **Denise, 41.** Jo's sister. Does the books on Tuesdays and Fridays, set up the office's cheap Remy plan, calls Jo "Joanna Marie," and is on Cal's side.
- **Kevin, 46.** Jo's ex-husband, a vice principal; decent and remarried. He shows up (every handoff, every game) and goes quiet in a fight: present in the room and absent in the argument, which is Cal the other way round. "Whatever works for you."
- **Vili, 29.** Jo's crew lead, nine years on her trucks. Talks. Wants his Saturdays off. Whistles two notes.
- **Manny.** Crew; falls asleep in the cab by the airport on the drive down.
- **Pat Dolan, 79.** Darlene's neighbor since 1974; kept the three boxes, the mail and a spare key.
- **Rita, about sixty.** Keeps Cal's house in Woodside and goes home at six-thirty (ch 17; Claude's addition).
- **Danny Zamora, 41.** Dawn's trainer; the only person who asks her questions (series seed).
- **Pancake, 11.** Cal's dog, named by Abby at seven. The 5 a.m. walk is the one thing Cal does himself. He leans on people: on Jo's leg in ch 5, on the glass where she stands in ch 12, against her shin in ch 14. He lives; there is one scare (ch 32).
- **Darlene Brennan.** Cal's mother. Born March 1954; left school at sixteen in April 1970 for the line at Libby's and told her son she graduated in 1972; died October 8, 2025, at 71. Never on the page alive; her house speaks for her.

Names rule (Todd): never use the name Priya. Avoid names AI fiction always lands on. Pick each name from the character's birth year and home ground, prefer plain to pretty, do not match a name to a job, and check it against the avoid list: Elara, Elena, Eleanor, Clara, Lyra, Mara, Maya, Kira, Marcus, Elias, Aris, Priya, Mateo, Theo, Leo, Kai, Finn, Silas, Jasper, Aria, Zara, Amara, Marisol, Dez; surnames Voss, Vance, Vex, Thorne, Chen, Hartley, Okafor, Sterling, Blackwood, Sharma, Baptiste, Marchetti.

## His list

Remy wrote it in nine seconds in April 2026, from fourteen years of his history, and he agreed with every line. The footer reads "Generated by Remy. Approved by C. Brennan." Jo knows from page one that a list exists and that she is not on it. Four lines are jokes, seven show his injuries, and one is true. The lines come out when they would in life, never one per meeting. Four are struck by chapter 7, all jokes; none in 8 or 9; the eight that are left cost him something and come one at a time from chapter 10. Each is struck on the back of his cue card in her carpenter's pencil, which will not rub out. She writes a line on the card only when she strikes it, and from line five on she strikes it only when he can tell her why it was on the list, so the card runs out of order: one to six, eight, ten, nine, seven, eleven (Claude's addition, October 10). He keeps the twelfth back. Dawn asked him for a thirteenth line, about what he would bring, and he had none; in ten years no client of hers has asked for someone who can say they were wrong. Jo writes the thirteenth herself in the epilogue.

| # | Line | What his history held | Jo | Comes out | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| 2 | Age 30 to 36 | Arithmetic | 44 | Ch 2 | Struck, ch 2. Funny |
| 3 | Dresses well | The same photos | Work boots and a clean shirt from the truck | Ch 4 | Struck, ch 4. Funny |
| 4 | Athletic. Works out most days | Control | Has never been in a gym; carries furniture for a living | Ch 7 | Struck, ch 7. Funny |
| 5 | College degree, minimum | His mother hid that she left school at sixteen, and read every report card he brought home | Two years at Skyline, then the trucks | Ch 14 | Struck, ch 14. His wound |

In 30 the envelopes make sense to him: his mother never accepted what he sent. Struck, ch 35, after he signs her note and takes her first check (Claude's addition). His wound |

## Remy and the plan

Remy is a long-running agent. It works for Cal around the clock, keeps a plan going for months, and comes back to him only when it needs a yes; he stopped reading what he approves in 2019 (three hundred approvals a day). Cal talks to it, reads it on a screen, and has it make songs, videos and drafts. What it makes is polished and could be for anyone. It can sound warm, and it is never wise: it reports, predicts and produces, and has no view on what is good. Cal calls it "it"; users say "he" or "she"; Janice says "Remy thinks"; Jo says "your robot."

- **It asks, and he says yes.** Remy never acts against him. It proposes, or carries on with what he approved, and he agrees or fails to stop it. Every wrong move is still his.
- **Doing nothing has to be chosen.** A running plan has to be stopped, and Remy tells him what stopping cancels.
- **It does not watch Jo.** It works from what is his (calendar, accounts, what he tells it) and what anyone can see (her booking page, her reviews). Her office runs on the cheapest Remy plan, which Denise set up; his Remy booked the first estimate with hers.
- **It delivers the promise and has no magic.** It schedules, books, sends, pays, builds and makes things, and never understands any of it. In the good weeks (17 to 24) it is off the page except for the sleep report (18), Owen's song (19) and the Napa card (20).
- **Four settings:** do it; do it if he has said yes before; ask first; leave it to him. Money always comes back to him to confirm. He walks the people he loves down them: in ch 16 he sets everything for Jo to ask first, and in ch 34 he sets Abby, Jo and Owen to leave it to him.
- **His record.** The first date is an anomaly ("no prior instances"), and he wants to be that man again. Every wrong move after it is the default his agent offers. He cannot change by deciding, because the agent records only what he does.

**The plan for relationship twelve.** Remy built it from his eleven relationships, which lasted 4.1 months on average. It offers it in chapter 6 and he says yes without reading it.
- It holds fourteen items: the set pieces of a courtship as his history ran it, among them a car at her curb, the month-two weekend in Napa (where the other eleven went) and a large gift at month four. The count only comes down: fourteen (ch 6), thirteen when he removes the car (ch 16), twelve when he cancels Napa (ch 20). From ch 16 nothing in the plan reaches her without his yes: the items between come to him one at a time as questions, and he turns each down without opening the plan. He will not open it because it would show him his history laid out, the 4.1 months, and he does not want to read what it predicts. The month-four gift is the last item (ch 28), and that one he says yes to.
- **The plan ends at the gift.** There is no item after it, because none of the eleven lasted to a month five. Remy never drafted a goodbye, and nothing in the plan was written to end things with Jo (Todd, October 8).
- **The nightly messages are not one of the fourteen items.** They are Janice's standing job. Remy keeps a list of the people she writes to for him (forty-one names); Jo's went on it May 16, and he takes it off on June 17 (ch 14).
- **How the other eleven ended.** Nobody ended them out loud. Each ended within two weeks of a large gift: the woman drifted off, and he let her go quiet, the way he took his mother's silences. That is why "Doesn't go quiet" is the true line, why Janice says he always gives a present before the end, and why Jo's nine days of silence in chapter 31 are the test. This time he keeps showing up.

What Remy puts in front of him, chapter by chapter:

| Ch | What Remy offers | What he does | What it costs |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | The end of the evening on schedule: his car at minute 45 and a call to get him out. Afterward the log: two missed calls, no prior instances. | Turns the phone face down. Cancels the other introductions and has Remy book the job she offered. | Nothing yet. He has found what he wants. |
| 5 to 13 | Janice's messages, as approval requests, most nights at 9:14. | Approves them. Reads none until June 5. | Jo falls for someone else's words. |
| 6 | "Relationship 12. Average duration: 4.1 months," and the plan. | Says yes without reading it. | The clock starts. |

**The second list:** what Remy may do for him in his private life. He takes it back item by item and hands one back at the worst moment. Ch 2 the end of an evening; ch 14 his words to Jo; ch 16 planning a date (no reservation and no wallet, because Remy and Janice have always handled both) and anything for her without his yes; ch 20 the Napa weekend; ch 25 fixing things for her; ch 26 paper in his name (he writes the two pages himself and releases the three women); ch 28 he hands one back (the month-four gift); ch 30 his apology (deletes the video) and his mother's voice (plays the voicemail); ch 34 his family; ch 36 the proof.

## Threads, clocks and plants

**The romance.** He falls on the first date and spends the book learning to stop managing her.

**The Sunnyvale house.** His mother's Eichler has sat shut for seven months because he cannot go in. It is in a living trust and he is the trustee, so nothing else was in the way. A crew could clear it in three days for about $4,500, and he would never see what was in it. Jo quotes ten Saturdays at $1,500 each, with a top price, because her rule is that whoever owns the thing is in the room and nobody stands in for him. Green tape goes with him; blue goes with her. Found items are called out, witnessed, written on the found sheet and signed for.

**Abby.** She moves into her dorm at Cal Poly on Tuesday, August 18. It is the school's first year on semesters, and Cal thinks college starts in mid-September; the sheet on his freezer door that he took for a schedule is the move-in date (ch 8). She stays with him from Friday, August 14, to Sunday, August 16, because he asked her himself in 22, and he cooks (Claude's addition).

Both clocks run out in the third week of August. Abby leaves on a Tuesday while Jo is not speaking to him, and the house is finished the Saturday after.

**In the house.** Frames still holding the stock photos they were sold with (Darlene liked those people). A sugar bowl with atrium gravel under an inch of sugar, so it always looked full (ch 10). Every gift he sent her, thirty-four boxes, unopened, with the gift receipts inside, stacked square by date in the closet across from the hall bathroom (ch 24). 156 envelopes of $612 marked "Calvin, house" with the month, $95,472 in all, in fourteen shoeboxes marked by year on the shelf above: her old mortgage payment, put by on the first of every month after he paid the mortgage off in 2012 without asking (ch 24). Height marks behind a robe on the back of the hall bathroom door: CALVIN 4 to 12 and ABBY 3 to 14 (ch 11). Her recipe tin, servings corrected from 2 to 1 (ch 10). Her high school withdrawal form under a drawer liner, and nine pairs of the same work shoe (ch 14). An upright piano under moving pads in the locked garage (ch 34).

## Chapter outline

Odd chapters are Jo's and even chapters are Cal's. Each entry ends on what has changed. Chapters 1 to 24 are drafted: the entry is a summary, and the chapter file is the final word. The full detail of each drafted chapter is in the Chapter log.

**Chapters 1 to 9: is there something between them?**

**1. Jo. Thursday, May 7. Drafted.** The first date, at the Pied Piper bar in the Palace Hotel. She is twenty minutes late because of a widow's recliner in Daly City, in work boots and a clean shirt changed in the cab, with her truck in a yellow zone on Minna Street. She knows his face from the "Call your mother" billboard on 101, and three more things: he is worth eleven billion dollars, he has a list, and she is not on it. He gives her twenty-five of his forty-five minutes, and she buys her own beer. She makes him put Janice's cue card on the table, asks for one line, makes him measure her with her own tape (sixty-four inches in boots), and writes the line on the back of his card in carpenter's pencil and strikes it. A stranger thanks him: "That is a room." "This is a table." "I am good at rooms." His mother died in October: "I sent things." At 8:45 his car is outside and his phone rings with a way out. **Changes:** he turns the phone face down.

**2. Cal. The same night. Drafted.** Line two goes on the card. Where people hide cash. The casserole dish and the silver. His last introduction asked him to rehome his dog, and he ended that date; Jo, bitten at 26, would never ask. Abby got into Cal Poly off the waitlist, his eyes fill, he blames the horseradish, and Jo passes a napkin and asks what Abby wants to build. Janice tracks him down on the bar's phone. Jo tells him his mother's house needs clearing and to call her office: a job, not a date. She leaves first, and he walks her to her truck because Janice told him to; her name is on the door. Remy logs it: two missed calls, no prior instances. From the car he cancels the other introductions and has Remy book the job she offered. **Changes:** "Eleven of the twelve are wrong. When is she free?"

**3. Jo. Friday, May 8. Drafted.** She tells Dawn it was fine and says no to a second date: it is not him, it is what he would do to her life. Dawn says he left her eleven minutes on her machine, with times in them. Sweet peas come, exactly right. Her office's cheap Remy plan shows an estimate his Remy booked at 11:52 the night before. At seven he is on the sidewalk in suede shoes, his back to his mother's house. He takes her no and will not ask again, and offers to take the job to another company; she keeps it. The house is in a trust and he is the trustee. She will not walk a house without its owner, and he cannot go in, so she prices it from the curb. He slips on the wet ramp and she catches his arm. Three days for $4,500, or ten Saturdays at $1,500 with him in the room. **Changes:** he agrees before she finishes the sentence.

**4. Cal. Saturday, May 16, the first Saturday. Drafted.** He is forty-one minutes early and sits in the car, in one of his forty gray shirts and new boots Janice bought. Jo stops short of the glass door, and with two people waiting he takes the pull. The stock photos in the frames. Pat Dolan wheels in three boxes he sent his dead mother by a standing order (Christmas, her birthday, Mother's Day); Jo reads the labels and leaves them untaped on the hearth. The garage door is locked, and he tells her it is empty; she writes it down. He cannot say green or blue. He stops where the kitchen floor starts: the yellow wall phone, and Remy's summary of her last voicemail. "Kitchens go last." Forty boxes and a tape gun; he cuts his thumb and she wraps it in green tape. Line three is struck when she looks down at her boots. **Changes:** she holds his wrist, and he sees her look.

**5. Jo. The same Saturday. Drafted.** The hall closet and the found sheet. He waters the dog from his cupped hands because the bowls are in the kitchen; she fetches a bowl and leaves the door open six inches. He has no lunch, and she gives him half her linguiça sandwich: "You can owe me." His mother's cafeteria badge from a coat pocket is the first thing he tapes green; she served him lunch, and he said thank you. He stays through the crew's lunch and decides the coats. The dog comes through the six inches and leans on her bitten leg, and she does not move the leg. That night a message, warm and exact, with a semicolon in it. She answers "Next Saturday bring a lunch," and gets back in ten seconds "Noted. That is five." **Changes:** she turns the phone face down.

**6. Cal. Saturday night, May 16, to Tuesday, May 19. Drafted.** Remy asks him to approve the message Janice wrote, so the reader learns who writes them; the ten-second reply was his. Remy files Jo as relationship twelve, average 4.1 months, and offers the plan; he says yes without reading it. On Tuesday he calls her office himself, moves a product review, stands in a bakery line in Little Portugal and brings her the wrong sandwich, a bifana, "wrong and good." The debt stands. Frank asks if he can carry, and he lifts a ramp on two. Someone has sent the widow with the recliner on the Alaska cruise she never got, with no name on it, and she thinks her dead husband sent it; Jo asks him one thing, did he ask her? "Next time, ask." **Changes:** "Is this where you ask me to dinner?" "No." "No." "I told you I would not ask again." She tells him to bring two lunches on Saturday.

**7. Jo. Saturday, May 23, the second Saturday. Drafted.** In the cab Vili says he thought she liked him. She does, and she keeps waiting for the piano. Kevin's silences; she never asked him for anything. Vili: she is listening for the load to move, and it is strapped. Cal brings three lunches, because on Tuesday she said next time, ask, and he asked her sister. She counts: he has followed every instruction she gave him, the no to dinner included, so he will never ask. She tells him: one dinner, and it costs him two lines; she picks the night, he picks the place. Line four is struck. His grandmother's hutch goes down the hall and up the truck ramp; "Low man calls the lift"; "Stop lifting my half." They are jammed together in the thirty-six-inch hall. **Changes:** she had started up onto her toes when Vili walked in.

**8. Cal. Wednesday, June 3. Drafted.** At home Abby is at the refrigerator, and he asks what she wants to build. "Who told you to ask me that?" "A mover did." Cafeterias, with windows. In Los Gatos he leaves Janice's card in the console. The room that was Manresa, now a members' club; Janice booked it. Her hair down, a green dress. Twelve tiny courses. The ten-million question. He gives her the keynote and she tells him he is presenting. Dawn asked if he wanted to be a husband; "I said I would get back to her." At course nine she is still hungry; "May I send for something?"; she bills it; he types one line and confirms an amount unread. He cannot pay the two lines: the eight left are not funny. No check; three twenties for the boy who brought the bread. She quotes a message he never read. **Changes:** "I noticed it recently." The first lie he tells her, at 9:14.

**9. Jo. The same night. Drafted.** A taco truck, Tacos Arreola, is in the club's lot, paid till eleven. She orders five al pastor, leaves a twenty for the cook, and tells him to feed the kitchen; Cal goes in and tells them himself. Janice's card has been in his car since 6:24, and he walks her to her truck because he wants to. They eat on the curb. Standing on it she is five foot eight. "May I kiss you?" "Hold still." "Cal. Come down here." First kiss. Her reasons stop at eight, and on the drive home she finds no piano. Denise, at the payroll, gets it out of her ("Did he kiss you?" "No." "I kissed him.") and finds her own name in the message from May 19. **Changes:** "I never told him your name."

**Chapters 10 to 15: can she trust who is speaking to her?**

**10. Cal. Friday, June 5, and Saturday, June 6, the fourth Saturday. Drafted.** He reads the nineteen messages sent in his name, eighteen for the first time, and learns Denise's name from one; Janice's footsteps line came from her own mother's house. He has kept the kiss from Janice. Jo backs the sixteen-footer into his car looking at him in her mirror. "Are you hurt?" He never mentions the car and leaves Remy's repair offer unanswered. Tongue-tied: "I am the one with thirty-four words." The sugar bowl full of gravel. Jo will not open the recipe tin and tapes it green without asking; inside, his mother's cards cut down from 2 servings to 1. On the bench their knees touch. His lunch was packed; next Saturday he makes it. She answers a one-star review under her own name, and he pays line six with the reason. She has carried her accident form since 7:04. **Changes:** he signs it and declines Remy's fix; she gives him the bottom copy.

**11. Jo. Saturday, June 13, the fifth Saturday, her custody week. Drafted.** Kevin has graduation, so Owen comes, under three rules; the last is that he asks a client for nothing. Under the billboard she checks her hair. Cal made his own sandwich, and she tells him to butter the bread. She takes $150 off the day. Owen knows the keynote to the second; his dog app has Pancake second. Behind a robe on the bathroom door are CALVIN 4 to 12 and ABBY 3 to 14, and he never knew about Abby's. Owen is half an inch over ABBY 9, and Cal writes OWEN 9 with her pencil; the door goes green. He asks her before he offers Owen the leash. "Bend down." "I have not asked whether I may kiss you." "I know. I'm telling you." Second kiss, in the hall. **Changes:** "Friend. Go ahead and ask him."

**12. Cal. Saturday night, June 13, and Tuesday, June 16. Drafted.** Owen's nine questions in the back yard. At 7:30 he tells Janice about the day and leaves out the door and the hall. On Tuesday Jo is in his building: Janice booked an estimate for her own office so she could look at her. He stops a gifts feature in one sentence, "It does not ship until it has a field for whether she is alive," and Jo is the only one there who knows where it comes from. Janice and Jo are polite in a way that draws blood; the card file has two cards on Jo. Owen sent two last questions: are his shirts numbered, and does Remy write his texts. "Remy does not write them." "Janice does." Twenty-three from Janice, nine from him; the dog, the footsteps, the sweet peas, Denise's name. "I will fix it." "How?" He has no answer. **Changes:** "Don't walk me to my truck."

**13. Jo. The same Tuesday. Drafted.** Forty minutes in his parking garage sorting the thirty-two messages aloud, laughing at "Noted. That is five," and sliding down in her seat when his dented car goes by. Dawn calls: in her divorce practice the men who sent the most flowers came in with a spreadsheet, "to show what she owed him"; "You tell him no, and you watch what he does with it." He is in her yard folding pads for Frank. In the sixteen-footer with the door at knee height she calls out all thirty-two and he names the writer of each; 23 and 9 go on a found sheet. "Which parts were you?" His seven, with no Janice or Remy in the sentence. He signs it. "There is no piano." "There's always a piano." The list: "You asked your app who to love." **Changes:** the contract holds, and anything he has to say before Saturday he types himself.

**14. Cal. Wednesday, June 17, to Saturday, June 20, the sixth Saturday, in Kevin's custody week. Drafted.** He takes her name off Janice's list, and Janice had already stopped. Eleven drafts, then four words: "I still owe linguica." Read at 10:04, no answer. He numbers his forty shirts himself; Abby can tell he wrote the message. A cold Saturday, and Jo keeps four feet off. Under a drawer liner, his mother's high school withdrawal form, April 1970, Grade 10, Employment. He pays line five with the reason: she told him she graduated in 1972 and read every report card before she signed it. Jo did two years at Skyline. Six lines are struck. Forty-five years on her feet; one pair of her work shoes kept. On the bench, twelve inches apart, the dog against her shin. **Changes:** at 9:14 she answers, "It's linguiça," with the mark under the c.

**15. Jo. Sunday, June 21, to Thursday, June 25, her custody week. Drafted.** He asks by text, in his own words, to work a load, and she puts him on her payroll: a client doesn't carry, and a man on her payroll does. Her side of his four words: she knew who wrote them by the last one, because Janice would have looked it up. At 5:12 a.m. he is in her yard; the new-hire packet goes on the hood, squared to the edge, and her crew shirt goes on him, SILVEIRA across his back. Her foolish act: she sends Vili in the other truck so Cal rides beside her. A two-story in Millbrae: a box on the shoulder going down, so you can see your feet; "Noted." "Seven." (the last count); the Delgados' son wants a picture and a pitch ("No pictures of my crew." "I work for her today."); his watch comes off at nine; the landing. He lasts five hours. At lunch he starts to ask in the stage voice, hears it, and asks in his own: he would not ask at his mother's house, where he is the client; Saturday after one, nothing booked, nobody told. **Changes:** she says yes before Vili can.

**Chapters 16 to 24: can their two lives fit together?**

**16. Cal. Saturday, June 27, the seventh Saturday, in her custody week. Drafted.** The morning at his mother's house is off the page. He plans the date alone, on one of his own cards in his own handwriting ("1:30 Jo. 2:45 Summit Road, wine. 5:00 Los Gatos, walk."), and tells Remy to book nothing; it answers "Nothing new will be booked," and he does not test the word new. At her curb is a black car he did not order, with her name and "the usual"; it has waited at eleven curbs before hers. "You didn't send it. You'd have asked me first." She tips the driver and sends him home, and they take her truck. From the cab: "Send no more cars for Jo Silveira." "Removed. Thirteen items remain. Review?" "I will look later." He does not want to read the plan; it would show him the eleven and the 4.1 months. Then: "Ask me before you do anything for Jo Silveira." Napa is where the other eleven went. Past Moffett, the zeppelin she watched from the cab in 2009 at $500 an hour; an investor took him up in 2011 and he answered email. Up 17 the reservoir is full, and on early jobs she has seen the crews on it ("That is the coxswain." "Is that what he is."). Up Summit Road from 17 they pass an apple orchard with an honor box ("I would not have funded it." "They didn't ask you.") and a small church, where Jo crosses herself without looking over. On Summit Road the villa has a wedding on its lawn and is members only by reservation; he will not be seated at a stranger's wedding, and the rental truck takes the sign. "Food first": tri tips from the case at the Summit Store, where his own setting makes his phone ask whether he may pay. He turns it face down and she pays. Then she takes him to Silver Mountain, up the ridge (Todd, October 11), where every house she moved up there told her to go if she ever got a Saturday ("Not till today."). The winery dog meets her door, and she stays in the cab until Cal asks, "Do you want me to keep him with me?" ("Keep him with you."). The whole of Monterey Bay lies below the deck. The owner, at the bar, talks about his land, his grapes and how he farms ("Because they aren't ready."). Cal tastes the Pinot with his thumb over its line, and she buys two bottles so the tasting comes off. She pays at the farmhouse on the way down too. In Los Gatos they park by the lake and walk the creek trail, her hand in his ("Don't tell me"; it was 104), to the dog park, where she watches the dogs from behind the fence. Line seven comes out, "Comfortable being looked after"; he cannot say why, so it stays on. **Changes:** "Come down here." He does not ask.

**17. Jo. Tuesday, July 14, and Wednesday, July 15, in Kevin's custody week. Drafted.** She walks an emptied house in Redwood City alone, garage last, with her overnight bag on the seat (the toothbrush out twice and back twice). "Guest room." "Yes." His house in Woodside is a three-truck house with nothing in it; she looks for the piano and sits in the cab a minute before she gets out. She will not let Rita, the housekeeper, carry her bag. Janice is in the kitchen with his cards for Wednesday's board meeting. The only thing in the kitchen anybody put out on purpose is his mother's cafeteria badge on the refrigerator, still in Jo's green tape. Offered halibut, Jo asks for a sandwich ("Calvin. The regulars order a sandwich."), and he makes it himself, buttered to the edge. Abby starts at Cal Poly "in September"; the schedule is on the refrigerator. A kiss at the guest room door, and she goes in by her own choice: she will not be the usual in his house. Her foolish act: at 2:10 she is at his door with her hand up to knock, and goes back. At 5:30 she says "I can do it" about breakfast, then sits on her hands and lets him (her second rung). It is terrible. At the sink she shows him where his own dish soap is and tells him about the down payment Kevin's parents never let them forget, every Sunday for eleven years. **Changes:** he promises never to pay for anything of hers without asking first.

**18. Cal. Saturday, July 18, and Sunday morning, July 19, the end of Kevin's custody week. Drafted.** Told to bring nothing, he brings nothing, and walks to the end of her street and back twice; the lady across the street calls her ("To give you one more lap"). She has vacuumed, and he keeps it to himself. Her kitchen is eleven feet and he takes up six; she moves him by the hips into the doorway, where Owen's height marks go up the frame in carpenter's pencil. Frank's linguiça on rolls does not count against the half he owes. He changed his shirt from thirty-one to thirty-two and back. He arranged for Rita to sleep at the house and walk Pancake before he was asked to stay. She leaves the pan in the sink. In her room he has decided to go slowly and not to look at his watch; she has her buttons undone before he has the first, and reads his label ("Thirty-one"). Nervous, he slips into the stage voice ("There's no hurry. We have all night. Tonight is about you."), and she tells him he is presenting. They laugh. She pushes him down and turns his watch face-in, and after that she decides. He finds the scar on her calf. She falls asleep mid-sentence about Vili. He wakes at 7:02. Her mortgage statement: $518,395.69 at 7.000 percent, 1,900.00 in the box for additional principal, NOV 2036 circled. She turns it right side up for him ("If you're going to read it, read it right"), and he checks her month in his head. She will be fifty-five and have a party in the driveway without the bank. He could pay it before she licks the envelope; he promised on Wednesday. **Changes:** his agent reports seven hours forty of sleep, no prior instances since 2019 ("I average five hours and eleven minutes"), and she pulls a chair out for him with her foot. "Sit down." He sits.

**19. Jo. Sunday, July 19, to Wednesday, July 22, in her custody week. Drafted, then rewritten on October 9 after Todd's notes.** At eight on Sunday morning, where 18 ends, Cal asks Jo first, before a word to Owen. Kīlauea has gone off fifty-one times since December 2024, the last time on Wednesday; the summit is swelling again, and Dr. Okamura will not promise. A plane on Monday, his house on Maui that he has never been to, the Big Island with a volcanologist, home Wednesday, and an airship. "You picked the zeppelin." "Yes." It is for her: he had it shipped on June 29, and she was always going to be in it. He could have said it was for Owen; he told her the part she could hold against him. Owen's question six, from the back yard in June, comes out through Cal, who has just read her statement: she has not had a day off since Owen was born and will take one when the house is paid. She says yes the way she can: she makes the lunches, for the crew too, and Cal asks Owen himself. Owen will decide whether it counts when he sees it; Haleakalā, seen from the plane, is asleep and does not. Kevin: "Whatever works for you." Vili does not get Saturday. Monday on Maui is her Pemberley (Todd, October 9). The house has no front door; you walk in and the ocean is the first thing you see. Gil, the caretaker, gives her the tour while Cal and Owen go down to the water: koa floors, walls that open, a bed on ropes nobody has been in, a library nobody has read, a pool with no far edge, good towels never wet, a refrigerator stocked every Thursday for six years, the week before's food to a food pantry (Todd, October 9: "It's donated to a local food pantry"; "We keep it ready. He's never been."). Cal bought it in February 2020 and was coming in April; through the shutdown he kept all six of the staff on full pay for two years, for nobody, and said nothing ("The checks came."). Over Gil's desk is the billboard, so they would know him; she has seen the other smile. On the lanai Denise texts *MARRY HIM*, and Jo finds out what eleven billion dollars is: a place kept ready every day for six years, so that the day you show up it is already yours. She lets herself have one minute of it (Owen every summer, her father on the lawn with his knees done, Denise in the pool in her earrings, no payroll, November 2036 gone with one word, herself asleep at seven), then finds the piano: you can't pay a man back for a house like that, and neither could your kid. On the beach Cal wears an orange vest ("I am a buoy."), Owen says the state fish's name all the way through, and a green sea turtle comes to Owen though he keeps the ten-foot law ("He came to me. That's allowed."). Owen draws the pufferfish nest in the sand, a wave takes half, "It makes a new one every time. That's the rule," and Cal says it back. No green flash at sunset ("It's resting"). Every good towel in the house gets wet. She sees the Milky Way for the first time; Cal knows none of the stars ("I have never looked."). He carries Owen in. "I have seen the invoices." He meant to come every April and had no reason that was not work; Owen asked about days off on June 13, and on June 15 he called about the zeppelin. Her own good towels at home have never been wet either; she does not say so. They kiss on the lawn and sleep in separate rooms. Tuesday: sandwiches at 4:30, twelve plus one for Gil, from groceries she bought in Kīhei with her own card, because the refrigerator is the pantry's. The airship in a pasture outside Hilo, nine of her trucks long; the governor and something in Hilo; what it looks like when the whole world says yes. The ground crew trades lead shot for passengers, the ground goes down, and it is quiet enough to hear a dog bark in Hilo with Owen's head out the open window. Mauna Kea's telescopes; the 2018 flow in Puna ("Nobody under there got to pack." "Some had a day. Some had an hour."); new land younger than Owen. A man on the highway pulls over to look up: that was her at twenty-seven. "You're missing it." "I am not." Over the crater the north vent is spilling red in daylight, and Owen says Halemaʻumaʻu right. Cal is airsick with her rag ("It is leased."; he answered email in the first one). At the rim the vent pistons all afternoon; at six the tilt is still rising, maybe tomorrow, and Owen goes quiet the way his father does. Cal calls nobody, promises nothing, and sits beside him; his watch has been face in since Saturday. At 7:40 the fountain goes (invented, Todd's choice; the episode is not named). The roar comes nine seconds after the sight, then the south vent goes: two fountains of about fourteen hundred feet, almost six zeppelins, lava rivers like the taillights on 101, a woman chanting, and Pele's hair on Owen's sleeve, which he gives back to the wind (his grandpa's ash was gray). Nobody paid for this. Jo sits down on the cold ground and puts her hand on Cal's back. "It counts." "It counts." It stops at 10:52 like a truck when you turn the key, and the rim applauds. "It did the whole week for that." Wednesday she sleeps until ten past seven, a first. Owen teaches Cal to float ("Your ears are the problem." "Now don't do anything."), and Cal asks Gil about his granddaughter Mahina's cast with no card (Todd, October 9: the softer side, away from his business; Frank stays home). The house looks like somebody has been to it; Gil: "Leave it. I've got something to do now." On the flight home Remy writes Owen a song about Pancake, and it is annoyingly catchy. With Owen asleep on his arm, Jo asks for line eight. "No kids. Wants them." She does not ask who wanted that one, and he does not move his arm. **Changes:** Owen starts a list, Volcanoes I Have Met, with one thing on it, and moves Pancake to first on the dog one. Truck Dog comes off, because he never met him: "It counts for everybody now."

**20. Cal. Thursday, July 23, and Saturday, July 25, the eighth Saturday, a hot day. Drafted.** The come-down from Hawaii (Todd, October 9: "There should be a come down from the big trip. Tell her sister all that."), deepened the same night so that Cal's interior carries the chapter between the exchanges (Todd: "Cutting and should in no way reduce interiorty. People don't speak with and yet have discursive thoughts."). At 4:58 on Thursday he turns his watch face up for a board meeting. Since Saturday night he had not known the time without asking someone, and for two he had not wanted to. On Maui it is Gil's Thursday, which Cal paid for six years without seeing. Janice has flagged eleven of 1,904 messages; none of them needs him, and she flagged them so that he would have something to do with his hands. "How was it?" He gives her the numbers. "Calvin." "Before it went, Owen did not talk for an hour. I sat on the ground next to him." She folds her glasses, which he has seen nine times in seventeen years. "Good." Abby told Janice, not him, that she is coming on Saturday. In the board meeting he taps Owen's Pancake song on the table and is back in the water on Maui, floating ("It held me up."). He last saw Jo at the SFO terminal on Wednesday night; she let him carry Owen to the truck and said, "Thursday's a workday." On Friday at 8:52 she texts: "Tomorrow, 7. It's going to be hot. Bring water for the dog." He tells her he missed her on Thursday. "Go to bed." Then: "Thursday was long." On Saturday he wears number thirty-eight and has decided not to bring up Hawaii unless she does. As the truck backs in, Vili says, "Forty minutes down 101, for a man who takes ten minutes on a lamp." Jo: "He's paying for the drive." Vili: "He's not paying for my Saturday." (Todd, October 9; the lamp is the one in ch 10.) Cal could pay for Vili's Saturday and keeps the number to himself; Jo pays Vili in the only coin he will take from her, the light end. Her nose is peeling, and he remembers her hand on his back at the rim for three hours. Owen is at Frank's, showing him the volcano videos; Frank has watched them six times and still hasn't said what he thinks. In Cal's old room his science fair ribbons, which his mother put up one at a time for nine years and he never looked at, go green. Nobody ever put air conditioning in the house. Denise drives down at 10:20 with three iced coffees, one for him ("I drive under your billboard every morning"), and he drinks it with milk and sugar. She asks Vili about Burlingame and not about Hawaii, and Cal reads it: she has come to see whether the business runs without her sister. "You texted me HOME. One word. After HAWAII. That's not a REPORT." At lunch he stays at the screen because he wants to hear what Jo says about the trip when he is not there, and he hears her tell her sister the plane, the house with no front door, six years of milk, the zeppelin ("It's not a blimp. It's got a frame inside." "He got sick." "He didn't throw up."), and the volcano that would not go. "What'd Cal Brennan do? Call the volcano?" "He sat down on the ground." "That's it?" "Kevin goes quiet in the next room. I say next time, bud. Nobody's ever sat down next to him, Denise. Me included." He turns his watch face in. He had been sure the airship was the thing; he sat down at the rim because there was nothing to buy, and for three days he had counted it as the part of the trip he failed to arrange. She slept till ten past seven while he failed to float. Denise: "Mom would've stood at that rope all night." Then the text: "I'm not going to marry a house, Denise." "Nobody said the HOUSE." He has a list for a wife and feels the watch counting. Pancake gives him away at the screen: "I have been here since the milk." Jo's face goes red, and she says "Hall." Denise arranged it, and he knows arranging when he sees it; Janice has done it for him since 2009. At her car: "Two more Saturdays after this one." "Then what?" "I do not know." "Find out." In the hall Jo measures frames she has already measured. "I should have gone to the living room." "Yeah. You should have." "I wanted to hear what you would say when I was not there." "Then you heard me say you threw up." "I heard you say I did not throw up." "You went gray." "I went gray." "You owe me one." "What do I owe you?" "I'll tell you when." He wants to kiss her, and he does not; she is on a job. Abby comes at noon, and she and Jo get on at once ("You're the one on the door. Three to fourteen." "My father used his hand."). Abby moves into her dorm on August 18, twenty-four days off; he had it as September and had been counting on it without knowing. Jo shows her the posts and beams and the copper pipes in the slab, and Abby tells Jo more about the house than she has told him in eighteen years. Abby takes the frame with the stock photo, the kite boy Darlene said looked like him at seven, and tells Jo that at seven she asked him how computers think only so he would talk ("Did it work?" "For an hour."). He had counted it as their best hour, and he does not want to check the other counted hours against what she would say. The podium photo falls and the glass breaks on the hall floor. Jo, who keeps a truck door between herself and every dog, tells Pancake to stay, gets down eighteen inches from him, and lifts the slivers with the sticky side of her green tape so he will not cut a foot. Cal sees it and says nothing. His photograph goes blue. Abby sees the bathroom door: her twelve lines, and OWEN 9 beside her nine ("Who's Owen?" "You were here for his." "Yes."). For most of her lines he was somewhere with a podium. Remy asks him to confirm month two, the usual weekend in Napa, at the inn he has been to eleven times and could not name; Abby reads it upside down, and she has watched eleven of these. "The usual." **Changes:** Abby, to him: "Don't do the thing you do." He cancels the one item; twelve remain, and he does not open the plan. He gets down on the hall floor beside Jo and holds the flashlight. "Lower."

**21. Jo. Thursday, July 23, and the same Saturday, July 25, afternoon and night. Drafted.** On Thursday, three days behind, she packs a dead woman's kitchen in Westlake in the fog while the son sits in the garage; she types Cal's name once and sends nothing. In the cupboard over the refrigerator a CorningWare dish has NORMA ABAD on masking tape on the bottom. She turns it over before she has decided to, puts it back by itself, tape down, writes nothing on the sheet and tells nobody (plant for 34). On Saturday, after the glass, she has not looked at him since the milk. At ninety-four in the hall he asks first: "I can have this house at seventy-two degrees by three o'clock. May I do that?" She says no ("Your mother lived in it hot"); his mother opened every slider at nine at night and put a box fan in his window. "All right." At one she sends Vili back with the truck ("We're behind"; they are ten minutes ahead), will take Caltrain home, and has packed two of everything without telling herself why; Vili hefts the cooler ("Heavy." "Ice."). She will not bill the afternoon. His old room: the bed made over and over, like the beds on Maui; a desk with no gum, pencils by lead size, CALVIN B on the calculator, a 2003 calendar blank after June. At three, ninety-seven in the hall, she lies on the slab and he lies down head to head: the red ribbon of 1997, lost to mold on eight kinds of bread ("The judges were parents."); nine batteries in one flashlight at eleven; "You haven't changed a bit." "She meant it as a complaint." He cannot decide the bed, so it stays. At six they eat her second sandwich on the bench and work till eight, and she falls asleep on the twin bed. She wakes at 9:47 under the bedspread; he is on the floor in the dark with the dog and does not know the time. Owen, at Frank's: "Grandpa says it counts." "There is still a train. I looked." She does not get up, and collects the one he owes her: line eight. "It was mine." After 2013 he had Abby every other weekend and Janice picked her up on Fridays; he has a video of Abby at two counting to ten and skipping six, watched 412 times; he wanted another child and to be there from the first day, with a woman who would not know what he did not do for Abby. "Do you still want a baby?" "The answer was no both times." He does not say Owen. She writes eight on the card under six and strikes it. "What do you need?" She cannot say it: "Come up here." The second love scene, in the twin bed: he takes her boots off, says nothing and does not present; she lets him take as long as he takes, and loses count. "I have decided about the bed. It is green." "It's a twin." "I am not complaining." **Changes:** she is in. Eight is gone, the piano on the lanai is still there, and she puts her hand flat on his back.

**22. Cal. Sunday, July 26. Drafted.** He wakes in his old twin bed with Jo's hand on his back and the watch face down, walks Pancake past the school where his mother went in by the side door at six (he told her he could cross a street by himself), and comes back to find that Abby asked Jo to the party herself ("She told me."). He asks Jo plainly, without the sentence he built on the walk; she asks who X is and what people wear ("I have not been to one."), and she rides in his car for the first time, asleep before Palo Alto, after reaching for her purse to pay for the gas and stopping. At home he sits over a blank cue card and leaves Janice's card in the present. Owen waves from Kevin's car. At Stephanie's house in Menlo Park, where he has been inside four times, Stephanie watches Abby's face when Abby sees Jo (his read). Jo's thirty-foot tape goes on Abby's pocket ("Thirty-six."), and his box goes on the gift table ("Thanks, Dad!"). Jo is easy with Abby's friends ("Not yet."). Until Abby corrects him his narration calls her mother X. "X wants the cake cut at seven." "Her name is Stephanie, Dad. You were married to her for five years. You can say it." He says it again with her name, and from then on she is Stephanie (Todd, October 9). At the cake a friend photographs Cal and Jo, and he lets her (it surfaces in 24). Abby squares his card on the table and points to the crossed seven in the date: she has known since she was seven who wrote her cards, and that Remy makes the videos. "You weren't a bad dad. You were a really good account manager." Jo holds his hand under the table. He answers with the list of what he did, in his stage voice: tuition since she was four, fourteen years of birthdays, her grandmother's house paid off. "That's three things, Dad. You did all three." He asks himself the question from 21 a third time and the answer is still no: he wanted to be at the kitchen table in 2010, and a baby would not put him there; the child he can still sit at a table with leaves in twenty-three days (Claude's addition, from the October 10 critique). He asks Abby himself to stay with him August 14 to 16; she asks who told him to ask, sets terms (he is home, he cooks, not Rita) and says "Okay" with no exclamation point (Claude's addition). In the car he gives Jo line nine, good family ("We're movers."); he cannot tell her why yet, and the card stays in his pocket. Frank is taking his boat out in the morning and said to bring him. Cal half makes a call for two racing sailors and decides that if he asks anyone it will be Frank. **Changes:** the verdict, from Abby; and Jo takes him to her father's boat. "He'll be there at five." "I will be there at a quarter to five."

**23. Jo. Monday, July 27, her mother's birthday. Drafted.** At 4:38 she drives under CALL YOUR MOTHER on the way to Oyster Point. Her mother would have been seventy; it is the one day a year Jo does not work, and Vili has had it in his phone since 2019. Cal has been in the lot since 4:26, on one headlight, in boat shoes bought for the 2014 retreat ("They have been in a box since then."); he walked the dog at 3:40 ("He was not in favor of it."). Frank comes at 4:58. Cal asks him first: two men who race sailboats, on the dock before five-thirty, paid for the day. "No." Frank hands him the cooler; his list has one line. At a minus tide (low at 6:12) Frank goes down the steep gangway backward with Jo one step under him ("I've got it."). The boat is a 1976 Catalina 27, worth about two years of slip rent, with the slip paid since 1994; most Sundays Frank sits in it at the dock after Mass, and he takes it out once a year. "Can you swim?" "I can swim. I cannot float." Frank tightens the life jacket himself. Frank's knees are the reason he gave; he could have put Jo on the bow, and he wanted a look at Cal. Cal hanks the jib on by Jo's flashlight and twists it ("Twisted."); Jo, crew on her father's boat, holds the light and says nothing. The blower runs four minutes and Cal counts. Out at 5:31 into fourteen knots of westerly, a wave goes down Cal's collar at the mast, and the yellow jacket comes out of the locker. Nobody sits in the corner by the hatch. Under the Bay Bridge at seven and the Golden Gate at ten to eight (slack 7:44), Frank takes his cap off; Cal looks at him once and then up at the bridge until they are through. Until 2019 Jo's mother rode under it in that corner, and all morning Jo had been afraid of what a man who did not know would say. Downwind on the flood Jo leaves her father the sheet she has had since she was twelve and sits beside Cal on the deck from Alcatraz to the Bay Bridge. In the gusts Cal takes the jib down; at the dock he puts the bow line on the back cleat ("Which one is forward?" "I watched a video." "Hm."). Frank asks Cal to dinner at six. Jo washes the boat alone and sits a minute in her mother's corner; Cal watches from the top of the gangway for forty minutes, then asks her: "Your father asked me. May I come?" "Six o'clock." At Frank's house, the one Jo grew up in, Cal holds the platter ("He's holding Dad's PLATTER"). Kevin brings Owen, shakes Cal's hand one pump, and thanks him for sitting with Owen at the volcano ("I did not do anything. I sat."). Owen: "Is Cal good at the boat?" "No." "Is he bad?" "He's new." Kevin knows the forward cleat is the one by the dock box; Cal squares his fork, and his ears go red.
