Denise: Dawn Pruitt called the office to ask whether Dad is seeing anybody ("Does she have a dog?"); Frank passes the bread instead of saying no. The cake comes every year, HAPPY BIRTHDAY CONNIE, one candle, and Owen blows it out. Denise: twenty-five years seasick under the bridge, "someplace famous"; Frank: "She liked the bridge." Jo says nothing; Cal's foot finds her boot, and he asks nothing about her mother. Vili calls at 7:20: the white truck's transmission went at one-thirty on 92 with a full load, and he waited because it was her mother's day. It had been slipping since May; Belmont tomorrow is two loads on the sixteen-footer; the repair is about four months of her extra mortgage payments. She tells Cal "Work." "All right." At Kevin's car, with Cal at Owen's window asking to see the one volcano again, Kevin offers Sunday night and she says her week is fine. "In eleven years you asked me for one thing. It was to sign." "You never asked me for anything either." "I was at every game, Jo." "My dad never came to one." She thinks of the water heater and still does not tell Cal about the truck. "You were jealous. Of Kevin." "Yes." "Over a cleat." "He knew which one." **Changes:** Frank, from the step, loud enough for the driveway: "Can't tell yet. Bring him back."

**24. Cal. Tuesday, July 28, after work. Drafted.** He drives his mother's meat loaf to Sunnyvale with the seat belt across it: the card from the green tin, serves 2 crossed out to 1, made for two. The first one went in the trash at 11:52 on Monday night (the card did not say to heat the oven first); this is the second. Abby comes in seventeen days, and he will cook every card with a 2 on it and make the mistakes on someone who will tell him. On Saturday Jo worked around the closet across from the hall bathroom and said it would wait for a weeknight with no crew. Both of them have known since May 23 what it holds: thirty-four slips, no boxes. He lets himself in, warms the meat loaf in his mother's oven, and does not open the door before she comes. Jo arrives at 7:14 in the sixteen-footer, dusty, after two loads in Belmont; he knows the white truck does a three-bedroom in one, and after her "Work" on Monday he does not ask. On 280 after Frank's he had counted the things Jo has told him to do since May 7, thirty-eight, every one an order. On the bench in the atrium: "It's dry." "Then the card's dry." "I have twenty-four cards with a 2 on them." Frank's "Bring him back" means Sunday or next July; "Then I will take Sunday." "Sunday he goes to Mass first. Then he sits in the boat." "What time is Mass?" He means it. She takes the end piece without asking; his mother always gave him the end, and he never knew which piece she liked. The closet: thirty-four boxes from the standing order, labels out, slips cut from every window, stacked square by date; shoeboxes on the shelf. "You knew." "I guessed. In May." He takes neither tape and opens all thirty-four himself, Jo calling and writing for two hours and four minutes: the same oatmeal cashmere wrap seven times on one item number, candles, robes, a blender, chocolates gone white, a photo frame loaded for her and never turned on. All blue but one wrap, green. On the shelf, fourteen shoeboxes, 2012 to 2025, and 156 envelopes, *Calvin, house*, each with thirty twenties, a ten and two ones; the last is *Oct 2025*. "Six-twelve. Was that her payment?" "Yes. I paid off the house in October 2012. She did not ask me to." To him it is cash that earned nothing for thirteen years. "She was stubborn." Jo knows what the envelopes are and does not tell him, as with the recipe tin; he had filed her paying back Kevin's parents under the same word. He signs the found sheet; green. **Changes:** on the slab at 10:31 Denise sends the photo from Abby's party, posted at 9:20 and captioned "Brennan's mover"; he knows who to call and leaves his phone in his pocket; her father's name is on both doors of the truck and the caption has only his; she puts the phone face down on the slab between them.

**Chapters 25 to 29: can he stop using money as love?**

**25. Jo. Tuesday night, July 28, to Thursday afternoon, July 30. Drafted.** On the slab at about 10:35 she likes the photo; the caption is the trouble. "I know who to call. It could be gone by midnight. Do you want that?" She does not. "Read me the two words." "Brennan's mover." "I've been Silveira's mover for twenty years." He could have the caption changed, and it would still be their page; he would like the whole thing gone, and she knows why (2013) and does not say it. He tells her what he can and cannot stop: the post, not the copies, and by morning people will find her company. "The company page is mine." Neither wins. Wednesday at 6:10 Denise has 206 new reviews on top of 431 ever; nobody is on the street; the white truck is $7,600 and three weeks, four months of her extra payments; her father has the sixteen-footer's keys before she gets there, to drive the loads in Burlingame (for 27). At 6:21 Cal asks before he does anything: Remy can report the reviews not written by customers. She says no ("Everybody who writes about us gets an answer from me. I sign it."). Alone in a dead man's den in Millbrae; Kevin's text: a mom at camp asked Owen whether she is dating Cal Brennan. At 11:58 she is 312 behind of 403 and cannot tell the made-up ones from people. He texts at 12:04, and she reads him the San Mateo mother, whose crew never stopped for lunch: "A model wrote that." "Can you?" (her third rung). "Bring nothing." He brings the dog, reads all 312 himself on her laptop with no Remy and no money, and writes the nine people on her estimate pad: Glenda Ruiz and the mirror; Rowena, who edited her May review ("Saw the picture. Silveira Moving is still the only company I would let in my moms house."); and Gary Roth's one-star, which she earned and answers first. He leaves at 2:41 to walk the dog at five; she wants him to stay and does not ask a second time. Thursday at 12:50 a courier brings fourteen pages from the Office of C. Brennan: a confidentiality agreement that binds only her, her employees and her family, $250,000 a breach, every page footed "Prepared by Remy from 3 prior agreements. Approved by C. Brennan.", and on page nine another woman's name left in, Ms. Holcomb. It is the first she has seen (Dawn does not use them), and she reads it as the bill for asking. In his garage at her tailgate: "Did you read this?" "No." He would have it withdrawn; she wants one, on her terms: two pages, his name wherever hers is, his people and his robot in it too. **Changes:** "Mutual," I said. "Or nothing."

**26. Cal. Thursday, July 30, 5:38 p.m. to about nine. Drafted.** On the shoulder of Skyline outside Castle Rock State Park (the gate locks at sunset; Jo reads the sign and parks outside it), she reads his two pages on her tailgate with the pencil. He wrote them himself that afternoon, 2:20 to 3:15, to her four sentences from the garage, and cut four lines to make two pages. On Wednesday at 6:24 Remy had asked about the old agreement, and he said approve without opening it, one of 306 that day; this afternoon he read it, read the three it came from, and wrote and mailed three letters releasing the women who signed them, Ms. Holcomb first. Jo is not told. Section four names Remy: "Does it sign?" "I sign for it." She strikes "family members" in her sentence (her father after Mass, Owen, "nobody's ever signed for Denise"), and he strikes his ("I cannot sign for Abby." "Good."); she changes her "employees, agents" to THE COMPANY PAGE and leaves his robot in; both initials on every change. The number: "It is twenty-three millionths of what I have." "That's forty-two months on my house." She strikes it and writes in the margin IF EITHER OF US BREAKS IT WE'RE DONE; since 2013 every agreement of his had a number in it, and this one has none. One copy, in her glove box. He bought the shoes himself at REI with the receipt left in the box, and she pays to the cent in cash; she carries the crash pad. On the way up she had stopped at Alice's Restaurant, where 84 crosses Skyline, for two burgers ("Named after the song." "It was named for the woman who owned it. She bought it in 1960." "What do I owe you?" "A waterfall."); on Goat Rock the foil says BMW on his and JALOPY on hers ("It's got Wagyu." "Don't get used to it."), he finishes his before she has hers unwrapped, and she gives him half of hers. At Castle Rock she measures the cave ("Five by eight"); he spots her, hands up, thumbs in; she is better at it, and he gets up the third problem by putting his feet where she put hers, with her spotting him. The falls are a dry streak ("It was a waterfall in March of 2009." "You quoted me a waterfall off a job you did seventeen years ago."). At Goat Rock at 7:58, No Service. She asks for a blue ribbon: a coin sorter, 1995, fourth grade, $53.60 from the jar on the refrigerator, "I put it back sorted," run twice "to hear it again"; "She asked whether I had washed my hands." Sunset 8:18; at 8:19 he tries the plain word. "I love you." She puts her hand on his chest: "Say it again when your heart's not going like that." He says it again across the hood of her truck, with his pulse normal, and she kisses him. He stands still with her forehead on his chest and waits for her to say it; his pulse is up again. Four times before Saratoga Gap she takes a breath through her mouth, and each time he holds his so he will hear her over the engine. She has not said it back. On 84 the signal comes back, and he asks Remy how the other eleven ended: each within fourteen days of a large gift, no final conversation; twelve items remain, review? Line ten, Never married; Jo was married eleven years. **Changes:** he closes the screen.

**27. Jo. Friday, July 31, to Sunday afternoon, August 2. Drafted.** At ten past four on Friday her father backs the sixteen-footer to the dock and gets down sideways, both hands on the door (one move on Wednesday, two on Thursday); she keeps her boot on the pads, because if she went over he would let go of the door to wave her off. "Monday." "Foster City. Two-bedroom." "Five-thirty." "I've got it, Dad." "I know you do." She has no driver for Monday, and he will have the keys off the board before she gets her coffee. Alone in the cab for the Friday truck check: his dock cushion gone flat on the driver's seat, his loads in pencil beside the tides (three, two, two), 191 miles, the ibuprofen half gone; three days of her loads on his knees so she would not have to ask anybody for a driver. She lifts the cushion back under him. Denise: Hillsborough (Wednesday, the big truck, $4,860) has stopped the check, *given what's being said about your company online*, and cancelled Saturday; Jo pays Vili and Manny for the day anyway ("Vili says THANK you"). "CALL him." "Don't call him, Denise." "Then who's paying for Dad's KNEES?" With a Saturday she did not ask for, she remembers the dog beach from a 2018 job in Carmel (*Dolores 4 NE of 12th*), where she stayed in the cab, and texts "Tomorrow 6. I'll pick you up. Bring the dog." "Down." At 5:55 he has worked out the coast from "down" and the dog: "It will be ninety in Gilroy by two. You would not take him anywhere that hot." The fog on the coast until eleven is why he brought a fleece (Todd, October 15: "Why would he think there is fog?"). On the drive he turns his whole head every time she opens her mouth, and all she talks about is the fog. Why she does not say it back (Todd, October 15: "We also need to understand why Jo isn't replying"): she wanted to. On the rock she wanted it said with his heart going normal; at her hood she got that and kissed him instead. On Wednesday she asked him for one thing, and saying it back would be asking for all of him. Then Denise would call him about the white truck, he would ask nice, and she would say yes; there would be a new truck by Monday with her father's name already on the doors. Over 84 and down Highway 1 in the fog ("Nobody takes 1 on a job."). At Carmel Beach she parks in the lot at the bottom of Ocean Avenue, where everybody parks, with a bathroom and a straight shot down the sand (Todd's); she stands at the top of the slope with her keys in her fist; surfers wait in the fog; Pancake, off leash, runs the waves in the wind with a spotted dog and a little white one and never gets farther from Cal than a sixteen-footer's length; his jeans in two folds ("Each one is three inches."); a loose V of pelicans and a seal watching the dogs; he rinses a piece of blue sea glass and holds it out ("It is blue." Blue is rare, about one piece in two hundred and fifty; she asks if he is a beach glass expert, and he read up on glass for the rock tumbler his mother bought him at nine), and she, who takes nothing home from a job, puts it in her pocket (Todd's idea). A black dog comes at her, and Cal steps to her left without asking and stays there; she takes his fleece. On a driftwood log under the bluff at the north end, below the golf course (Todd's), she tells him about Kevin's silences (the salt, Tuesday to Sunday, "what time he'd be home Thursday") and her half: one thing in eleven years, and when he went quiet she painted the hall, twice in one year. He gives her line ten: a woman who had been married would have known how his marriage ended; Stephanie told him she was lonely, he said he would fix it and had Janice book a counselor, he went to two, and in March she filed. She thinks of the white truck. "Card." She writes ten under eight and strikes it. "What do you want?" The house comes to her mouth, December now; what she wants is him on her left without being asked and no piano after, and she shuts her mouth on both. The fog comes off the bay, the water blue all the way to Point Lobos and the beach one white curve, and the Pebble Beach golf course is right over their heads; a ball comes down off the bluff, hits the log a foot from Cal's hand, and Jo throws it back up overhand onto the grass, and the golfer lifts his hat to her (Todd's); it is a long walk back down the beach on soft sand, Pancake slow in front of Cal, and he shakes on them halfway up the slope; Cal clips the leash on at the top, in the lot (Todd, October 15); at the truck Jo has a towel, a gallon of water, a mixing bowl and soft treats in the bed, packed Friday night because he is old; she dries Pancake on her knees on the asphalt, starting on his legs like table legs before the pads go on; he will not stand still and wiggles the whole time, nose to tail, turns all the way around to get his back done again, gets her in the face with his tail twice and leans on her shoulder for his toes, the longest she has ever had her hands on a dog, and he takes a treat off her flat hand; Cal crouches across from her ("Thank you." "He was wet.") and holds the dog's head while she wipes his face (Todd, October 15: "Jo should show some considerate for Pancake"). She takes them out Scenic Road past houses with names instead of numbers, a stone tower one man built in four years ("Bet his knees were shot."), and the Mission, where she crosses herself (Todd's route). At Point Lobos, where a dog may not go even shut in a car, she parks in the turnout and Pancake sleeps off the beach; Cal reads her the trail guide ("It is an alga."); where the trail comes out at the edge of the point the cove is beer-bottle green and clear to the kelp, the swell goes up white higher than the trees, an otter sleeps on its back in the kelp; "A painter called this the greatest meeting of land and water in the world"; she has wrapped it a hundred times over couches in Atherton and figured somebody made it up; she names the sea lion that never moves Manny, and on the narrow trail she puts both hands flat on his back, he holds her wrist there, and she waits for the piano (Todd's stop). South of Point Lobos the road on the side of the mountains, gold hills with cows at angles she wouldn't park a truck at, nothing on Cal's side but air and the water, his arm out the window; she has lived three hours north of this all her life and never driven it. At Bixby she pulls in with the thirty people ("It is two hundred and sixty feet down to the creek."): the arch, the creek crossing a gray beach into water the color of a swimming pool, headlands going bluer to the sky; every load she ever ran south on 101, this was over the hill; Cal is on her left and she did not see him get there. Then the wind at the top, the Point Sur light on its rock, the redwoods in the valley and out into the sun (Todd, October 15: "It's awesome. Make people feel that."). At Nepenthe she goes to the rail before the stand: ridges going south bluer and bluer, waves too far down to hear, "I've stood on a lot of decks in Hillsborough that thought they had a view." (no dogs; at twelve minutes he goes down to check the truck) a man shouts "Call your mother!" with his phone up; the last picture of them cost her $4,860 and a Saturday, and she stands between Cal and the phone ("No pictures. He's on his lunch."); Cal's hand goes from his knee to her back. She reads the menu from the right. She has had coffee at five and nothing since, and Cal has been looking at the next table's burgers. "May I get lunch?" "I've got it." On Monday at five-thirty her father will have the keys. Two Ambrosiaburgers with coleslaw and a Red Seal each (one beer; she is driving), $81.50 before tax, "I could do eighty-one fifty," paid in cash, five twenties; the juice down her wrist; the jay gets a piece of his roll. "May I get the cake?" Four-layer chocolate fudge, $16.25: "It was cake." "Yes." He cuts it down the middle and turns the half with more frosting to her, and he pays for the cake (Todd, October 15: "They need to eat more than fries at napenethe. It's been a lot of walking, they are hungry, the have a drink. Maybe the Four-Layer Chocolate Fudge Cake."). Then Cal, who looked it up while she paid, asks "May I get a room?" at a hotel back up the road that takes dogs (Alila Ventana, Todd's). A night there is most of Hillsborough, but it is one night and she can see the end of it, so she pays for the dog ("The dog's mine."), $150 on her own card and the chicken and rice off the dog's menu, which Cal reads to him. They have no clothes: at Nepenthe he has clothes sent down from Carmel and asks to send some for her ("I've got a shirt behind my seat." "All right."); at six the boy in the vest brings two shopping bags (a shirt, pants, socks, a sweater and a toothbrush, each folded in tissue) and a bag of the old-dog kind of dog food, which Cal sets by the bowl for the morning; "Somebody had driven fifty minutes down 1 with a toothbrush."; she gets her own shirt from behind her seat (ch 1) (Todd, October 15: "They don't have any close. he would have clothes delivered" and "And dog food."). She tells the valet the truck's seven foot ten with the rack; Pancake lies across the door, not on his bed; dinner came with the room, and she said yes to the room; the fog comes in underneath them until there is only white with the ridges standing out of it, the sun goes down into it, the stars come out, and she puts her hand on his in front of everybody; he turns his hand over and waits, her face goes hot, and she passes him the bread (her father's dodge in 23; she sees it as Kevin's salt in 31). In the dark her mouth on his neck and her hand on his chest where it was on the rock, his heart going normal, and she could have said it then. She has it in her mouth: if she said it back she would be his to fix, paying him back past fifty-five. She shuts her mouth against his shoulder. On Sunday the menu on the desk has no prices ("I didn't know which side to read it from."); breakfast comes on a cart at eight and is set out on their deck over the canyons, coffee in a pot, orange juice with pulp on the glass; they eat in the hotel's robes, "as thick as a moving pad"; the fog still down in the canyons, the ridges in the sun, the ocean coming out blue past the last ridge; something in the oaks sings the same three notes over and over; Pancake, fed from the new bag at seven, on her bare feet; eggs and bacon, the lox on a bagel "because it was there," a warm roll under a napkin, "Cal had the same as me"; one piece of his bacon to the dog; two gray squirrels around an oak trunk, Pancake lifting his head to watch, and they watch for a whole second cup; his robe open at the collar; twice he looks at her over his cup like he has something to say and drinks his coffee; she starts stacking the plates and he puts his hand over hers (Todd, October 15: "They wouldn't leave at 6, they would have sumptious breakfast with coffee and orange juice and every luxury on the veranda over looking the canyons." and "In lux robes. Birds singing. Watching squirels."). They leave at eleven and come home up 1 and he sleeps sitting up; at San Gregorio she does not turn up 84 for Woodside but takes him home for coffee, and the glass goes on the sill over her sink. **Changes:** on Sunday afternoon he reads *skip Aug* upside down under her circled NOV 2036, does not ask, and puts both hands around the mug; she hears *I said I would fix it* again and turns the statement face down.

**28. Cal. Monday, August 3, to Friday, August 7. Drafted.** "On Monday it had been four days since I told her I loved her. I opened her thread nineteen times that morning." The first is at 5:04 in the morning at the fence post by his gate (every morning at five he and Pancake walk down the drive to the gate and back; nobody has ever asked for a meeting at five, ch 4; the post's suspects over eleven years are deer, a mountain lion one summer and, for two years, a white standard poodle three drives down, and he has not put his own nose to it), under a moon most of the way full, with Pancake stiff since the beach; the last thing in the thread is his *Understood. We will be at the gate at 5:55.* She told him when to say it and he said it; what came after, she had not said. On Sunday she turned *skip Aug* face down. He carries Pancake up the last three steps. Janice: a shirt, pants, a sweater, socks and a toothbrush delivered to Big Sur, and dog food. "One toothbrush?" "She had a shirt behind her seat." Remy at 11:52: month four begins Friday and the last item is a gift; "I will decide on Friday." Fourteen days from Friday is August 21, between Abby's move-in (18) and the last Saturday (22). Tuesday at 9:14 at night she calls, and waits out a plane over her house (eleven seconds): Owen wants to take him up Sweeney Ridge at seven ("He told your robot."). "Pancake cannot walk four miles anymore. I will tell Owen myself." "Everybody moves in August." He sat at the end of a table with eight people for six hours and opened her thread twenty-two times: "Our general counsel noticed." She laughs. He wants her in his kitchen and puts his hand flat on the stone. He has the sentence ready; a third time, she would have to answer it. Tokyo is his own idea, made in fear (the plan sent the eleven to Napa): one night at Ventana taken as a number to build on; Kevin has Owen from Sunday; leave Tuesday the eleventh at nine, land Friday the fourteenth at 10:40, Abby at five; fourteen trips and nothing seen; Fuji mostly hidden in August; four days somewhere he could see everything coming. He holds the plane and a suite ("It would cost nothing unless we used it."). Wednesday at 6:52 in the evening, Skyline College, Lot 2: Jo gets out in his fleece from Saturday, zipped to the collar. "I parked here for two years. Class at eight, the yard by one. Never went up." (She looks at one space.) Owen brought the mixing bowl from Carmel for the dog; told no, he puts it back and goes quiet, his father's quiet; Cal could have had the dog driven up the Sneath Lane road with two calls and keeps his phone in his pocket. Coastal scrub, sharp and medicinal: "Smells like my dad's knees." Old foundations (Owen walks out onto them: "Owen. Poison oak."; leaves in threes going red; he comes back the long way), the ravine she takes sideways like a loaded ramp, her hips in his fleece. At a mile and a half the Nike radar buildings: "They had missiles up here." "This was the radar. ... The missiles were on Milagra Ridge." "Remy said missiles." "It is a missile site. The missiles were somewhere else." "That's misleading." "I will tell them on Monday." On the paved road Owen tells Cal why he asked: in June his mother said she would come up when the house is paid, so he told her Cal wanted to see where they found the bay and had Remy tell him all of it, "so it'd be a real thing." "You came, though." At the Discovery Site the fog lies on the ocean side level with their boots and comes over the edge in pieces that are gone ten feet down; the bay is silver from the airport both ways, with the San Mateo Bridge, Diablo, and Tamalpais over the fog on the city; four hundred takeoffs with his laptop open, and never up here; his mother's house thirty miles south in the haze. Owen: almost four months from San Diego, lost and sick and hungry, looking for Monterey, walked right past it; the Ohlone from Pacifica walked them up; "So it doesn't count." "You've been busy." "Remy did the dates." Jo looks at Cal. "The dates were right." Jo leans back against his chest; he counts her breaths to nine and stops, because he is not counting to anything; he knows what to do with a yes and a no, and not with the time in between. Eight planes in a line over the bay; a wide-body climbs west into the fog. "May I take you to Tokyo for four days?" "You've got a plane." "I am holding one." "Holding." "A hold costs nothing until it is used." "I can't leave on a Tuesday." "May we leave on Wednesday?" "I can't leave on a Wednesday either." "Do you really need to work this much?" He hears it after he says it. "Yes." Her hands close into fists in the pockets of his fleece and the back of her neck goes red from the collar up; he does not know what he has caught her at. She steps out of his arms. It is line eleven, *Doesn't need to work. Free to travel.*, said in his own words on a ridge with his arms around her. Sunset at 8:13; "Our house is right there." "One of those." The section ends: "She would take a day off when the house was paid. I knew what she owed on it to the cent. I had known since July." Thursday at 12:46 in the afternoon Denise calls with her turn signal going ("Don't tell her I called."): the transmission on 92 on the twenty-seventh, $7,600 and three weeks; Hillsborough's $4,860; Frank driving since Wednesday and getting down SIDEWAYS; no extra on the house, the first skip since 2023 ("I do her TAXES."). "I will not keep this from her." "I didn't ask you to LIE. I asked you not to say I CALLED." "Those are the same thing." At the yard at 4:06, wind fifteen knots gusting twenty-three, Frank gets down sideways, good leg first. "Denise called me. She asked me not to tell you." "Course she did." "May I get you a truck?" "No." "May I pay for the transmission?" "No." "What is wrong?" "I've got it." (He has heard it over the fourteen-dollar beer, about her bag at her curb, and about lunch; each time about a thing he could see.) Frank hands him the other end of a pad: "Corners." "Back to the wind." "Half." "Walk it in." "Again." Forty-eight pads, every pad on the truck; Jo at the office window turns away from the glass; "You fold square." At 10:02: *Your father and I folded forty-eight pads.* Delivered. Friday at nine in the morning Remy: month four, the last item a gift, eleven prior large gifts averaging $31,400, the largest to one person October 2012, D. Brennan, mortgage paid in full: "What is the gift?" Fourteen days from today is August 21. He sits with his hands flat on the desk until 10:06 and types her lender, her loan number and her July balance; the lender takes a payment to a loan from anyone but gives the payoff figure only to the borrower, $99.42 a day, and a wire of $521,000 with the loan number in the reference comes to about $4,500 more than the real payoff once her August payment is in, the excess refunded to her and the lien release mailed to her. Janice at 10:19: "It's a gift, Calvin. You can't even deduct it." "I am not trying to deduct it." She pauses before she answers; most days she has his answers ready a day before he needs them. "Calvin. Ask her first." "I asked her twice this week. She said no both times." "To this?" "I won't process it." Remy: "You can confirm it yourself. Confirm?" Between meetings the reasons come after the decision: November 2036 moved to December; four months of extras for a transmission and two and a half for Hillsborough; her plates on the deck; her father's knees; Owen's question six and her three days in Hawaii; Sweeney Ridge, where one evening was what Owen could get her and he could get her the rest; the party in the driveway in August, at forty-four; 2012 from the back of a car, and the shoeboxes ("She was stubborn. Jo was stubborn too."); in 2012 he wanted his mother to stop making a payment, and he wants Jo to have her party in August; eleven gifts he could not name, and this woman's loan number by heart; a volcano nobody can make go, and a number he can; the promise at his sink, asked on a ridge and on her dock. At 2:51 that afternoon he types *May I* under the pads and deletes it. Her silence is never given as the reason. **Changes:** at 3:12 he confirms the payment. At 3:30 Janice gives notice by hand, last day Friday, August 28, dated 8/7/26 with the seven crossed ("She had picked the twenty-eighth. Every dial in her office sits on an even number." "One. ... Two."). When she is gone he opens Jo's thread: "The last message in it was mine."

**29. Jo. Wednesday, August 12. Drafted.** "At twenty to five on Wednesday morning I stood in my driveway in my socks with a cup of coffee, looking northeast, because my son told me to." Owen's text from Kevin's at 9:40 the night before (*Perseids tomorrow morning. No moon. Look northeast when you get up.*); no fog for once, fifty-seven degrees, his fleece zipped to her chin, the motion light next door; four minutes, a plane into SFO, four more; one meteor across the whole sky, a box cutter going down the tape, green at the very end; "There," to nobody; *Saw one.* At her counter, doing her bills before the yard: the September statement has not come; she turns the old one face up for the loan number (*skip Aug* in her pen, for the check she'd mail in August, under the NOV 2036 circled twice) and checks the last extra went where she told it. *Principal balance: $0.00*, a green bar, *PAID IN FULL.*; *08/10/2026. Payoff received. $521,000.00.*; the excess, $4,483.45, refunded to the borrower. The refrigerator; her shoulders come down. The order matters, because many readers think a paid mortgage is the dream, so the reader gets her reason before her anger (Todd's core, October 16): at seven, the Millbrae house with green carpet, the man in the car at the curb all morning, her on her back in the square where the couch had been with nobody needing one thing from her, the horn, the car gone; at sixteen, on 280, whose house it was; she walks every house last, by herself, and has never told anybody why; her father in a chair the winter she was eight; Owen five weeks old the first time Kevin went quiet; "I don't lean on anything that can go." Since May she has done it anyway, her shoulder on his arm from Alcatraz to the Bay Bridge. "A house I pay off myself can't go quiet on me. It was the one thing I'd let myself want. I was going to have it at fifty-five. Then I was going to lie down." On Monday somebody carried everything out of hers, and she knows who; his piano came in by wire with her loan number on it, and from now on every time she comes in her own front door she walks into something he gave her. At a minute past five, the bank (eight back east): Rochelle, "Congratulations!" "I didn't pay it." "Can you send it back?"; a payoff can't be reversed from her end, the sender would have to recall the wire with his own bank; the letter in two weeks, the refund in thirty days, the reconveyance in thirty to sixty. She was going to make this call in 2036 and laugh. The fleece comes off, over a kitchen chair. The spreadsheet: 156 lines (the number on Darlene's found sheet), 33 dated, 32 with the extra, *55* in the notes on the last; every line from September down had been hers. VOID across the check; the statement back face down, where it lives till the next one comes. Then the promise from 17, at his sink, said back word for word; he keeps what he says to her. The pads text, six days unanswered: "Every answer I had started with thank you." "I wasn't going to say this on a phone." The sea glass on the sill is black. At ten past six, the yard under low cloud: Denise ("Is it HIM?" "What's on the calendar?"); Janice Louie called HERSELF, moved her office up to the twenty-EIGHTH, wants it walked again at four and asked for Jo, whose plan had been Woodside tonight: "Four o'clock was sooner." Owen at 7:02 ("Only one? Where were you looking"; the peak is tonight; "Good. Look northeast."). Westlake, her second since Maui, pink, the fog down on the roofs; Sharon up from Bakersfield, her father dead in June at ninety-six; Jo takes the hall closet herself and the whole time is at her counter looking at a green bar. The ninth picture: 1971, the mortgage burning over a barbecue in the driveway, the street in lawn chairs with highballs, Sharon at twelve with her hands over her mouth; two hundred and forty payments; it was a copy. At half past twelve her father gets down sideways; she does the number on a driver for his cab and won't: "Every one of those dollars had Cal's name on it." At ten past three she stands in the square where Sharon's father's couch had been and hears only Rochelle. At five to four, his garage, level two; sixty desks and every head up; Janice's office in boxes, 2009 through 2026; the cabinets go twenty-two feet into his office, full, every card out and back in with him in the room. Janice: "I didn't send it." She told him to ask Jo first; he sent it himself at 3:12; she gave notice at 3:30, the rest of her year in that office given up over Jo's house. "I've sent all the others." "What others." "He always gives a present before the end." "The end of what." "That's his to tell you." The slips (7, 24); "He'd said it to my face on a rock, with his heart going under my hand. I hadn't said it back." She can't tell which kind she got. On Janice's desk, the list, all twelve lines: line eleven is the question he asked her on the ridge, and he has fixed it so she doesn't ("*I said I would fix it.*"); beside line twelve, in Dawn's ink, *5/4. Joanna.*, three days before the bar. "Which wall do they go on?" His wall is nineteen feet. "May I tell you why?" "You read my statement upside down." "Yes." "You promised me at your sink." "Yes." "You didn't ask me. You knew what I'd say." "Yes." Dawn's "watch what a man does with a no": two no's on her dock, forty-eight pads, then $521,000. "I wanted you to have your party in August." He thought the party was about the bank. Only then her anger, exact and never cruel: "My name was on that loan." She has more, in order, and under it the word she did not say on the rock; one more word might be that one, and that would be putting her whole weight on a man whose own assistant has just told her he always gives a present before the end. She does not decide her silence caused it, and the book does not say so either. **Changes:** she shuts her mouth. The estimate to Janice; "May I walk you to your truck?" goes unanswered; her boots the loudest thing on his floor; he stands in Janice's door with his hands at his sides, waiting to be told where to be; the doors close.

**Chapters 30 to 35: can she lean on someone who could go quiet?**

**30. Cal. Saturday, August 15.** Abby is staying with him that weekend, as she agreed in 22, and he is cooking (Claude's addition). He keeps the terms: something from work asks for him that weekend and he says no, and he cooks, badly, from his mother's tin, the cards she changed from 2 to 1; on each one he makes he crosses out her 1 and writes 2 (Claude's addition). Abby answers what he does, not a speech (critique of 22, October 10). Janice is working out her three weeks (28) and does only what he asks: for the first time since Abby was four there is no present on the table in his front hall and no card written for him in his hand (22), so he writes Abby's card himself, at his counter, and it is short (outside critique of 28, October 15: losing Janice should cost him in practice; Claude's addition). In 22 he told her he paid off her grandmother's house. Abby: "You did it to Grandma too." At the house Jo is there, because her father's name is on the contract, and she speaks only to Vili. The hall bathroom door is green, so Vili pops the hinge pins ("Doors come off," 11), and Jo has it loaded without a word to Cal; it goes to Woodside with the other green things. That night Abby finds it in his front hall, CALVIN 4 to 12, ABBY 3 to 14 and OWEN 9, and stands against it and has him mark her, ABBY 18 (outside critique, October 13; the mark is Claude's addition). The envelopes make sense to him now. That night Remy offers him a new list, and it is Jo, line by line; he closes it without saving. Remy offers the usual next step for an unanswered message: an apology on video, in his own voice, better than he could give. He deletes it, and asks what else is planned. Nothing: the month-four gift was the last item, and no prior relationship reached month five. For ten months he has read Remy's summary of his mother's last voicemail. Tonight he plays it. **Changes:** his agent: unanswered, three days.

**31. Jo. The week of August 16.** Her side of the silence. Denise, who has a key to Jo's house and reads whatever is on her counter, has taken the sea glass off the sill over the sink (27) and brings it back wrapped in wire on a chain. She puts it on Jo herself, and Jo wears it from then on (Todd, October 14: "I want the Denise to make a necklace out of the sea glass and give it to Jo and she wares it." The placement in 31, the key and the sill are Claude's; Todd left the call to Claude, October 14, 11:24 PM). She is not punishing him, and she knows nothing about his mother's nine days. If she opens her mouth, the next word may be the one she did not say on the rock, and that would be leaning her whole weight on him (29). She is putting her old payment into an account every month, exactly as his mother did. Her father, who went quiet on her twice (the winter she was eight, and 2019), asks whether she has ever let anyone take the heavy end, owns the 2019 silence in a line, and tells her that anyone can face a crisis; it is daily life that wears you out. She sees that she is doing to him what Kevin did to her, and what her father did; that is the difference between them: they never saw it, and she will end it herself (ch 34). Nobody says it. On Monday, August 17, the day before Abby leaves, Stephanie, Abby's mother, comes to her yard unannounced, in a car that does not belong under the INDUSTRIAL CITY sign: the book's Lady Catherine (Todd, October 9: "Ex wife is good."). She was married to Cal from 22 to 27 and has watched the eleven since: the Napa weekend, the large gift, the woman drifting off. Abby liked two of them, and neither one said goodbye to her. Abby has told her about the mortgage. She asks Jo to let it end the way the others did, quietly, so Abby can leave on August 18 without one more goodbye that never comes. If it does not end, she has brought a fallback (Todd, October 9: "The ex wife might want the new wife to sign a comprehesive prenup, she's worries about abbie."). She sets an envelope on Jo's desk, square to the edge. "When I married him, nobody asked me to sign anything. There was nothing to sign. He's known you three months. He paid off your house. The next thing he pays for is a ring." It is a premarital agreement from her lawyer, forty pages. Jo reads every page while she stands there. In it Jo gives up his company, anything he earns after the wedding, any support, and any claim on him when he dies. Abby's trust stays whole. Owen gets nothing. Jo agrees with all of it. Schedule A is his, four pages. Schedule B is hers, one line, blank. "That's for you to fill in." Jo fills it in. "The house in San Bruno, and what I owe on it. Silveira Moving & Estate, three trucks, my father's name on the doors since 1987." It runs off the line and down the margin. She puts it back in the envelope unsigned and hands it back. "You agree with it." "Every page." "Then sign it." "Nobody's asked me anything. If he does, he can bring it himself." Then Stephanie asks about Owen. "Your son ranks dogs. Is he ranking Cal?" She is right about the eleven. She cannot know that this time he did not go quiet. He was at the house on Saturday, and he will be there on the next one (critique, October 9). Jo will not promise. She says it exactly and without cruelty: she will talk to him when she is ready, and not on anyone's schedule. It gets back to Cal through Abby, with the envelope (32). Cal sends nothing and fixes nothing. She remembers Ventana (27): his hand turned over under hers, waiting, and she passed him the bread. That was Kevin passing the salt. She has not said it back, and she knows now what it is like on his side of the table. **Changes:** nine days.

**32. Cal. Monday night, August 17.** That evening Abby brings him the envelope her mother took to Jo's yard, then goes back to her mother's for her last night; he is to drive her down in the morning. From Abby he hears that Jo would not promise to let it end. Jo read every page of the prenup, agreed with all of it and signed none. Her side is filled in, in her printing, down the margin. He does not understand one line, "and what I owe on it," until 34 (Todd, October 9; that Abby brings him the envelope is Claude's, so that he has it for the epilogue). Pancake collapses. At the emergency vet at 2 a.m. the one person he wants is not on his payroll, and he calls her. She comes, sits on the floor beside him, and talks only about the dog. The dog lives. She is gone before he can thank her. In the morning Dawn calls from her trainer's gym, with Danny Zamora in the background asking her a question she does not answer (the seed for her book; Claude's addition). She ends him as a client, and tells him why she sent Jo (Todd, October 12, from the matchmaker's channel). She cross-examines him: how many before Joanna ("Eleven."), and which of them was more in love ("They were."). Then: "Have you told her?" "Yes. I told her twice." "Has she said it back?" "No." (Todd, October 15.) The rule lands on that. Then she gives him the rule her own therapist gave her ten years ago, at the end of her marriage (that it was her own therapist is Claude's addition). Only two kinds of marriage last: they love each other the same, or he loves her more, madly more. The one that fails is when she loves him more, because a man that far gone does the work (he listens, he changes, he gives things up), and a woman does the work either way. "I hated her for saying it." All eleven did the work, and he sent presents. Joanna was never going to be impressed by him; if it worked, it would be because he was the one who was gone. And she was the one his daughter would call if something happened to him. He hears which one Dawn was and asks, "Which one were you?" She does not answer it, any more than she answers Danny. He told Jo he loved her in 26 and went around her in 28; Dawn tells him that being in love was never the test, the work is, and that sends him into the letter in 33 and "Tell me" in 34. Then he drives Abby to San Luis Obispo. **Changes:** he has just found out who he calls.

**33. Jo. Tuesday, August 18, and after.** Abby sends Jo a photo from move-in day: her father on the low end of her drafting table in a stairwell, staying for the parent events. A letter comes in his handwriting with the list, annotated. Across the top: "I didn't write this. I agreed with it. That's worse." The letter leaves no secret: she was filed as relationship twelve; he approved a plan without reading it; the car and the Napa weekend were that plan; the plan stopped at the month-four gift, because none of the other eleven lasted longer, and the payoff was that gift; each of the eleven ended within two weeks of a gift, and he let each of them go quiet; he has asked Remy for nothing about her since. Jo writes a promissory note on her own terms: seven percent, the rate she was paying, her signature and a line for his. They are apart for the whole chapter. **Changes:** she is going to make him take the checks.

**34. Cal. Saturday, August 22, the last Saturday.** At eleven, when her crew stops as it does on every job (25), he has brought his own lunch for the first time: linguiça and egg on a sweet roll, in foil, made himself, not from Frank's linguiça (18) and not from a bakery (the bifana, 6). On the atrium bench where she tore hers in 5, he tears it in half and holds one out. She takes it. That is the half linguiça paid, and nobody says so (outside critique, October 13; placed at lunch, not before "Tell me"). The house is empty, and Jo does the walk-through she does before she gives back keys. Cal watches her stand in each empty room a little longer than the job needs, and does not know why; the reader does, from 29. The garage door is still locked, and her clipboard from May 16 still says what he told her: empty. Vili takes the door off its hinges. Under moving pads is an upright piano, his mother's, which he did not know she had (Claude's addition). In the bench is the payment book: she bought it in September 2012, on payments, for Abby, who was four and came every year (her marks are on the bathroom door; Stephanie brought her, and Cal never came, which is why he never saw it). Every payment is marked in her blue ballpoint through the last. He paid off the house that October and never knew. After Abby's visits stopped at fourteen she kept it in the garage under pads. Nobody says the connection: he reads the date, and Jo sees him read it (outside critique, October 13; Claude's form of it). It was never on her estimate, and on any other job it would need a change order. She does not write one. She hands him the low end, the heavy end on a ramp, and lets him call the lift, and they take it up the truck ramp. She does it before either of them has said a word about August. In the empty living room he says, "Tell me," and she does: everything she is angry about, out loud, and under it, told short, what the reader has had since 29 and he has not: the Millbrae house at seven (the wife killed herself, five weeks after the baby; one line, no method), and the winter she was eight, her father in the chair, the casserole dishes and the names on the bottom, which she has never told anyone. He fixes nothing. They finish the house. When it is empty she lies down on the slab in the hall, where she lay in 21, and he lies down beside her without being told: the picture at seven, and this time she is not alone (Claude's addition, October 16). The chapter carries a lot; keep each beat short (cold review, October 16). The gifts go blue; the slips were already green. The three boxes are still on the hearth with no tape on them, the last decision in the house. He opens one and does not know what it is, because he never chose it. That is where he tells her what line twelve meant, the nine days and the report card on the kitchen table that ended each of them, and the cafeteria: at that school he was the lunch lady's son, and he made sure the other boys did not know it, so he thanked her like the woman next to her. She asks for the card and writes nine and strikes it. Line nine's meaning has changed, and it is not a contest between families: he put it on the list to marry away from being the lunch lady's son, and by now he knows his mother was the good family he had, and that Jo's is one that carries for each other (critique of 22, October 10). She does not strike twelve; it is the true one (Claude's addition). He has been polite and quiet ever since, and it felt like being safe. The work sits between her story and his, so neither comes straight after the other (critique, October 9). **Changes:** his agent that night: no precedent found. He changes one setting: for Abby, Jo and Owen, Remy does nothing unless he asks.

**35. Jo. Late August.** She texts him the way she did in 18: a time, and "Nothing." He comes to her door with the dog and nothing else (critique, October 9). She is wearing the glass (31; Claude's addition). She hands him the note: his gift cannot be given back, so it becomes a loan only if he agrees. The note is her way of taking the dish back washed. Darlene put his money in envelopes and never said a word. Jo started the same way in 31. The note is the difference: she says it to his face and makes him take the checks (critique, October 9). He signs the acceptance line, takes the first check, and cries without blaming the horseradish. He can tell her now why line seven was there (30), and she writes seven on the card and strikes it (Claude's addition). Getting a glass, he opens the wrong cupboard in her kitchen and finds a recipe tin. He holds it shut in both hands, the way she held his mother's in 10, and puts it back where it was. He does not ask, and she does not explain (Claude's resolution, October 13; the tin is never explained to anyone). Third love scene: he is present and controls nothing. She says it, and it answers the rock in 26, where she made him wait for his heart to go normal: "I took his hand and put it flat on my chest. My heart was going so hard he could feel it. I didn't wait for it to slow down." "I love you," I said. He says it a third time with his heart going, and she does not make him wait (Todd, October 15). It is the first time she leans on purpose on someone who could go; the note still lets her pay the house off herself (October 16). **Changes:** "I need you to stay."

**36. Cal. September.** Proof of change. In September Jo put his name on Owen's emergency card, third, after her own and Kevin's, and she asked him first. The card is the list of who to call when something bad happens; it takes the house's place as what keeps her safe (October 16). He is on stage at the fall keynote, on a school day. The chapter opens with him at full power: four thousand people, the thing he does best, and it is going well (October 10 critique: more human without becoming less formidable). Then his phone rings. It is Owen's school: Owen is sick, Jo is two hours away on a job, and Kevin, a vice principal in the middle of his own school day, does not pick up. Nothing is queued, because he set it that way. He takes the call in front of four thousand people, tells them he has to go, and goes himself, with no driver and nobody to ask. He has no idea what a sick nine-year-old wants, gets half of it wrong, and stays the night. It should read as an ordinary bad day, not a final exam. Janice has retired and left him the card file; he writes a card for Owen himself and draws a pufferfish on it, badly. That night Jo writes eleven on his cue card and strikes it (Claude's addition). **Changes:** Frank: "He carries okay."

**Epilogue. Jo. A year on.** The same hotel bar, forty-five minutes booked, and no car comes. First he puts the envelope from 31 on the table, as she told Stephanie he could. Her side is still in her printing. The one line he has added to his four pages is her note, paid to date (Todd, October 9). On the page this takes three sentences, and no terms are read. Then he hands her a cue card in his own handwriting. Under ASK it says one thing: her. Under DON'T: say "noted." There is no car time on it. The cue card is the proposal. It comes after the envelope, so that it is the last thing (critique, October 9). The list is on her fridge with eleven lines struck in carpenter's pencil and a thirteenth in her handwriting, about him: says when he was wrong. The twelfth stands, and she uses it. **Changes:** she takes the pencil from behind her ear and writes her answer on the card, where it will not rub out.

## Chapter log

One row per drafted chapter, kept so that no detail is used twice. Each row gives what the narrator wants, what is in the way, what goes wrong, what cannot be undone, the details used, the running counts and the new facts. Where a row says something is Claude's addition, it waits on Todd unless he has ruled on it.

- Ch 1, Jo. Wants: a beer with nothing to win, and to be home early. In the way: he gives her a time limit and a list she is not on, and she likes him. Goes wrong: his car comes at 8:45. Cannot be undone: she lets him measure her and strikes a line off his list in pencil that will not rub out; he turns his phone face down. Details used: the $110 valet; a yellow loading zone; a clean shirt behind the truck seat; tape-gun callus and cardboard cut; the twenty-five-foot tape; the flat carpenter's pencil; sixty-four inches in boots. October 8 pass: one beat added after he turns the phone face down (the living rooms where sons check their phones over their mothers' things; her stomach goes over the hill again).

- Ch 2, Cal. Wants: to work out why he stayed. In the way: she fails every line and he can find nothing against her. Goes wrong: his eyes fill in front of a stranger; Janice tracks him down by phone. Cannot be undone: he cancels the three introductions and books the job at his mother's house. Details used: cash taped inside a closet door frame; the casserole dish and the silver; his watch reporting his pulse; a telephone on a tray; the bar closing at eleven; green tape on her thumb.

- Ch 3, Jo. Wants: to say no and keep her life as it is. In the way: two machines booked him into her morning, the flowers are right, and her pulse has not heard the no. Goes wrong: he takes her no without a fight, so she has nothing to push against. Cannot be undone: ten Saturdays with him in the room; he says yes before she finishes. Details used: the Remy Starter screen; four dozen pads to a truck; a change order for the piano; sweet peas in a coffee can; the hillside sign in fog; the sixteen-footer and forty minutes to Sunnyvale; the black car at the curb; his back to the house; suede shoes on a wet lawn and then on a wet aluminum ramp; the key in an envelope, offered and refused; green and blue tape; $4,500 against $1,500 a Saturday; a top price. Running counts: his "noted" is at four, her reasons at five. New facts: Denise does the books Tuesdays and Fridays and calls her Joanna Marie; the sixteen-foot truck is kept back for estimates; Owen is at Kevin's until Sunday; the estimate was at the curb of the house and she did not walk it (he could not go in, and she does not go through a house without the owner); he first sees her yard in ch 6. October 8 pass: one beat added after "nothing to push against": "I had wanted him to argue. I found that out on his mother's wet lawn at seven in the morning, and I didn't care for it."

- Ch 4, Cal. Wants: to get through the first Saturday as a competent client. In the way: the house, a neighbor with three boxes he sent to a dead woman, and a kitchen he cannot walk into. Goes wrong: he cannot say green or blue, and he stops at the kitchen floor. Getting in (added 2026-10-05, since ch 3 says he cannot go in): Jo stops short of the glass door and will not open it; with two people waiting on him he takes the pull, and his hand lifts the sticking door from habit before he decides. She says only "Runners." Cannot be undone: Jo has seen what "I sent things" means; line three is struck; she holds his wrist and he sees her look. Details used: forty-one minutes in the car; 15.4 miles and four visits in five years; $21,500 in 1960 and a watch that cost more; thirty-one shirts tried and forty bought; boots bought by Janice; the front door that opens on a courtyard; the watered maple; stock photos in two of nine frames; slab, no attic, no crawl space ("she hid at standing height"); eleven doors, nine sliding; the shipping label; recycling never broken down; the locked door to the garage, "It is empty," and her note (added 2026-10-08 as the piano's plant); the key on red yarn; the aluminum strip; one cup on the drain rack; the yellow wall phone; the wide street, the church on the Bernardo corner and the school across from the house; his mother handing him a lunch tray; the H of tape on a box bottom; a box in six seconds; the tape gun's teeth; green tape on his thumb. Running counts: her reasons at six (said aloud; he does not know what she is counting); his "noted" still four ("Understood" is "'noted' in a different shirt"). New facts (ch 4): Darlene died October 8, 2025; the voicemail is 47 seconds, left October 6 at 2:14 p.m.; the standing order has run since 2014; her birthday is in March; Pat Dolan has lived next door since 1974; he drove himself.

- Ch 5, Jo. Wants: to keep the first Saturday a job, and to eat lunch with glass between her and the dog. In the way: a man watering a dog from his hands, no lunch of his own, a badge in a coat pocket, and the dog. Goes wrong: the dog comes through the door she left open and leans on the leg that was bitten. Cannot be undone: she fed him and he owes her; he told her about the lunch line; he taped his first green; she let the dog stay; she answered the message. Details used: fourteen coats hung by length; the found sheet (called out, witnessed, written, signed); nine peppermints, $1.37, a hairnet, two church bulletins; forty-five minutes of lunch, unbilled; eating apart from the crew; the hose bib and cupped hands; mixing bowls left of the stove; six inches of open door; linguiça and egg on a sweet roll, since the Hillsdale exit; her father's key; the badge (SUNNYVALE SCHOOL DISTRICT / FOOD SERVICE / DARLENE); tape torn badly; eleven stitches, a garage in Pacifica; cereal at the counter; her cell on the paperwork; the semicolon. Running counts: her reasons at seven; his "noted" at five, and he counted the fifth himself; green items, one. New facts: Frank makes her sandwiches on Friday nights and has a key; Jo has cleared two hundred houses; the first message came at 9:14 p.m. from his own number, and the instant reply was his own. October 8 pass: one beat added after the badge: she wants to put her hand flat on his back, between the shoulders, the way you steady a man on a ladder, and keeps it on the clipboard.

- Ch 6, Cal. Wants: to pay a debt himself. In the way: he does not know what he owes (the wrong sandwich), her father, and a gift he sent without asking. Goes wrong: the cruise cannot be fixed, because the widow thinks her husband sent it. Cannot be undone: he said yes to the plan unread; he did not ask her to dinner, and she noticed. Details used: the approval request; three hundred approvals a day, unread since 2019; eleven minutes to Janice at 7:30; the number on her truck door; a moved product review; nine people over seventy in a bakery line; no wallet; a car with 1,100 miles in three years; the whole hillside sign; the bifana; the thumb; Frank's kitchen chair and tide book; a fourteen-foot ramp, palms up, lifted on two; sovereign wealth funds; one sandwich for eleven; five stars, eleven sentences; four fixes, each worse. Running counts: his "noted" at six; her reasons still seven; the debt of half a linguiça stands. New facts: Janice has written his messages since 2009; the plan has fourteen items; Mrs. Bautista is eighty-one and was married forty-one years; Frank calls her Jo and she calls him Dad; he works out four mornings a week.

- Ch 7, Jo. Wants: to stop waiting for the bill, or to find it (the dinner is her going to find it; ch 8 answers with a room that has no check). On the page Jo never says "the bill"; her word for the catch is "the piano," the thing that is never on the estimate (Todd, 2026-10-06: abstract money talk sounds like Cal). In the way: Vili's questions, her own answer about Kevin, and sixteen inches of hallway. Goes wrong: she says out loud that she never asked Kevin for anything. Cannot be undone: she asked for the dinner herself; line four is struck; she had started up onto her toes when Vili walked in. - Ch 16, Cal (drafted 2026-10-08, rebuilt from Summit Road on at Todd's notes the same afternoon; not yet line-reviewed). Silver Mountain Vineyards sits at 2,100 feet on a ridge over Monterey Bay, is open Saturdays noon to five, welcomes picnics and has solar panels on the winery roof; its founder bought an abandoned 18-acre orchard there in 1979, came through the early-1980s floods and landslides, the 1985 Lexington fire and the 1989 earthquake that destroyed the winery, farms organic for less fruit with more flavor, and holds his reds five or six years (Entrepreneur, 2009); the estate Chardonnay started in 1981 from Mount Eden cuttings put straight in the ground with no rootstock, and he is planting more Bordeaux as the climate warms (Edible Monterey Bay, 2022); its Pinot Noir and Chardonnay have won medals; Wrights Station is a 1947 farmhouse with vines against the redwoods, up Loma Prieta Avenue just past the store (Edible Silicon Valley); the Los Gatos Creek Trail runs from Vasona north to the Los Gatos Creek County Dog Park, which has small- and large-dog sides (Wikipedia); Todd's local corrections (Regale has the wedding lawn; one stop at the Summit Store during the tasting; tri tips from the case; Los Gatos a good twenty minutes down; the reservoir full; October 11: the tasting at Silver Mountain instead of the schoolhouse winery, with its view of the bay, mountains and vines and its Chardonnay and Pinot; a church and an apple orchard on the way up; the owner at the bar, blunt, talking about his land, grapes and growing; a winery dog that owns the place and that nobody takes by the collar; you pay after tasting, and buying wine takes the tasting off, her idea). The first draft followed the record (at dark the north vent glowed and Owen ruled that it counted); Todd rejected that draft on October 9 ("The house bland. There is no pemberly moment for Jo. There's no [wonder] in the zeplin flying. No majesty in the volcanoe.") and asked for the tour, the sights, play on the beach and Jo thinking about what she could have. The pufferfish moves from the rim to the sand on Monday. Checked: episode 51, July 15; episode 52, July 28 to 29 (USGS; Hawaii Guide); episodes began December 23, 2024 (NPS); episodes open with gas pistoning, spatter and overflows from the north vent, then sustained fountains, and in 2025 the north vent reached 800 to 1,000 feet in an hour ([Big Island Video News](https://www.bigislandvideonews.com/2025/05/16/klaueas-episode-22-lava-fountains-reach-1000-feet)); later fountains reached about 1,500 feet from both vents; witnesses describe a jet-engine roar heard before the fountain is seen, rivers of lava across the crater floor, a plume lit from below, an end like a closed valve, and cheering ([Darker View](https://darkerview.com/wordpress/?p=30087), [Adventures NW](https://www.adventuresnw.com/?p=24446)); Pele's hair is golden volcanic glass spun out by the wind; about 700 homes were lost in the 2018 lower Puna eruption, which filled Kapoho Bay with new land; a Zeppelin NT is 75 m (246 ft), with two pilots and twelve passengers, big windows, one opening window a side and a curved rear window, and it hovers on its engines for boarding while the crew exchanges weights ([Bay Crossings](https://www.baycrossings.com/airway-to-heaven/)); green sea turtles off Mākena and the federal guideline to stay 10 feet away; Molokini's crescent off south Maui and Kahoʻolawe behind it; Haleakalā is 10,023 feet and last erupted about 500 years ago; Mauna Kea's telescopes stand near 13,800 feet; a first-quarter moon on July 21, 2026; Hawaii is three hours behind Pacific daylight time; the summit is about 4,000 feet; Portuguese sweet bread and sausage are everyday food in Hawaii. Places at full size (Todd, October 15, 6:49 AM: "the pure awe of being in a zeplin over a volcano and the beauty of the ocean and house and the island and the wildlife and of Jo"): the water turning dentist's-fish-tank green over the reef as they come in, foreheads on one window; the mountain that keeps going up; the warm wind through the house, "I've never been in a house that let the weather walk through it," her bag on the koa; the drop-off past the reef, sun in long ladders, forgetting Burlingame; Jo up the sand last in the Target suit, Cal stops on his buckles and watches her all the way up, "I could feel exactly where he was looking," no towel; the sun down behind Lānaʻi, gold to peach; Haleakalā pink behind them at dawn and the Big Island ahead "like a loading ramp"; the shadow nine trucks long; her face out the window, ʻōhiʻa tops red "like spatter off a roller," "one hand on my son's belt and nothing else in the world to carry"; the crater's heat in the window, the airship lifting "like my father's boat lifting on a wake"; spinner dolphins on Wednesday, one spins three times, "Cal opened his eyes and sank." About 7,900 words. Checked: the July 20 sunset bears about 292 degrees, behind Lānaʻi from Mākena; ʻōhiʻa blooms in scattered tufts; spinner dolphins are seen off south Maui year round.

## Dates

Every dated fact in the chapters must agree with this section.

**Before the book**
- 1947 Pat Dolan born. 1952 Frank born. March 1954 Darlene born. July 27, 1956 Connie, Jo's mother, born (ch 23).
- 1960 Darlene's father buys the Eichler new, $21,500 (ch 4).
- April 1970 Darlene, sixteen and in Grade 10, leaves school to work the line at Libby's; her father signs the withdrawal form (ch 14). 1974 Pat moves in next door (ch 4).
- 1980 Kevin born. November 1981 Jo born.
- March 1985 Cal born. Libby's closes, and Darlene goes to the cafeteria at the school across the street, where she stays thirty years, to 2015. Denise born.
- 1987 Frank founds Silveira Moving (ch 2). Winter 1990: Frank hurts his knee on a job and the one truck sits until spring; the church and the neighbors feed the family, and a man from the parish pays the truck note for three months. Jo, eight, washes and returns the casserole dishes and writes down every name on the tape on their bottoms (told to Cal in ch 34).
- 1989 to 1997 Darlene marks Cal's height on the back of the hall bathroom door, ages 4 to 12 (ch 11).
- 1994 Cal, nine, asks once about her schooling, and she tells him she graduated in 1972 (ch 14). Frank buys the 1976 Catalina 27; every year from then to 2018 he takes Connie out under the Golden Gate on her birthday, seasick in the corner by the hatch, and Jo has the jib sheets from twelve (ch 23).
- 1996 to 1999 Cal at the school across the street; his mother hands him his tray (ch 4, 5).
- After high school Jo does two years at Skyline College, then the trucks need her (ch 14). 2003 Cal leaves his mother's house (ch 10). 2006 Jo is on the trucks full time ("twenty years," ch 5).
- 2007 Cal marries at 22. April 2008 Abby born. 2008 Jo bitten on a job at 26, eleven stitches (ch 2, 5).
- 2009 Janice starts, when the company is nine people. She has written his messages since, started his cue cards, and keeps a card for everyone he meets (ch 6, 12).
- 2011 to 2022 Darlene marks Abby's height on the same door, ages 3 to 14; Stephanie brings her, and Cal is not there for any of them and does not know (ch 11; who brought her settled October 13).
- September 2012 Darlene buys an upright piano on payments, for Abby, who is four, and pays it off herself; after Abby's visits stop she keeps it in the garage under pads (ch 34; Claude's addition).
- 2012 Cal divorces at 27, and Remy's fourteen years of his history start (ch 2). October 2012 he pays off his mother's mortgage without asking, and she starts the $612 envelopes. Jo marries Kevin.
- March 2013 a woman he has known four months sells nineteen hundred words about his custody hearing, with Abby's school in it; Abby is four, and he moves her (ch 10).
- 2014 the standing order for his mother's gifts starts (ch 4, 12).
- 2015 Abby, 7, names Pancake. Cal tries thirty-one shirts and buys forty (ch 4).
- February 2017 Owen born. Abby is nine that year (ch 11, 12).
- 2019 Jo's mother, Connie, dies, before her birthday (ch 1, 23). Since then Frank takes his cap off under the bridge, Vili keeps the day in his phone, Jo does not work on it, and Denise orders the cake. Cal stops reading what he approves (ch 6) and takes the chairs out of his reviews (ch 12).
- December 31, 2022 Manresa closes; the room reopens in 2024 as a members' club.
- 2023 the billboard goes up (ch 1). Jo and Kevin divorce after eleven years (she put the papers on the kitchen table on a Sunday, after he had started talking again; ch 23), and she refinances at seven percent to buy him out. Cal buys the car he never drives (ch 6).
- October 6, 2025, 2:14 p.m.: the voicemail, 47 seconds. October 8, 2025: Darlene dies at 71. The 156th envelope is the last, thirteen years exactly.
- December 2025, March 2026 and May 5, 2026: the three boxes ship (ch 4).
- April 2026: Remy writes the list in nine seconds (ch 2, 13). Dawn asks him whether he wants to be a husband (ch 8).

**Ages in May 2026:** Cal 41, Jo 44, Abby 18, Owen 9, Frank 74, Denise 41, Kevin 46, Janice 61, Dawn 54, Vili 29, Pat 79, Pancake 11. Jo is older than Cal, and that stays (Todd). Chapter 2 says he is 41 and was 23 when Abby was born; Jo was 35 when she had Owen. Owen is nine, the age Abby was in 2017, the stretch of her childhood Cal missed; chapter 11 uses it without saying it, and chapter 19 can use it again. Two soft spots: Cal's birthday is in March, so that "41" and "the year I was born" both hold in May; and the school may have been a two-year junior high in his day, so no chapter says how many years his mother served him.
